to him. They were reconnoitering as they proceeded along the road. When the figures had disappeared for the third time he turned swiftly aside towards the creek, and looked sharply round for some pla-Ce of concealment. The water was flush to the edge, and the tanks afforded no shelter. Hie was unable to swim and dared not risk the crossing. Then a few yards away he saw that one of the little gulleys narrowed up till it almost disappeared under the tall weeds on either side. He did not hesitate for a moment, but flung himself into it. From its narrowness he had estimated its depth at about two feet, but to his surprise he sank down and down until his head was under water and he felt the soft mud banks press into his shoulders. With a stupendous effort he dug his feet and hands into the mud and raised himself up to the surface, spluttering and gasping for breath. One of his hands had caught something hard, and he brought it up with him. As he held it above the water in the moonlight he saw that it was a gold watch and chain. He thrust it into his pocket. It represented an additional item in his small exchequer. He found he could just touch the bottom with his feet, and his head was concealed by a bunch of weeds overhead. As he felt himself sinking into the mud, he moved up'a little towards the rgad in the hope of finding a better footing. His feet encountered something firm, but elastic, like a pilloW stuffed with straw. He put one toe under it and gave it a lift. And then a horrible thing happened. He felt the object rise from the bed of the gulley, and a few seconds later a white face rose from the water. He moved aside the weeds and let the moon fall on it. It was ghastly, dis- torted, and streaming with black mud. A small crab was hanging to one of the ears. It was the face of Susanson.

"TIE BEATAI.L."

Jermy forgot all caution in his terror and shrieked aloud. And looking up at that moment he saw two figures on the bank against the sky. He knew he was discovered for they had descended and came hurriedly towards him. He' struggled out of the guiley and was on his feet to meet them before they reached the spot. He was a powerful man, and had no intention of dying like a rat in a sewer. As he rose from the water the white face of Susanson sank slowly back into its muddy resting place. . . . _ . .

The two niefi came to within five feet of him, and he saw that Jordison held a revolver in his hand. Then they stopped. "What are you doing here, Jermy ? " Jordison asked in a quiet voice. The man did not answer, but braced every muscle in his body. He had an idea that Jordison would not fire, and that it would come to a physical combat. "Well," Jordison repeated. " Yer bloomin' skunk ! " Lipp growled. `yer thought yer'd got the £5,000, eh ? " and he laughed horribly. Jordison raised his revolver, but Lipp laid a hand on his arm and muttered in his ear. The next second they were upon their victim, and the three rolled over on the spongy ground. The contest was short but decisive. Jermy drove his fists into Lipp's face with such force that half of the latter's front teeth went down his throat, and the next second he had bitten a piece out of Jordison's ear. But he was overpowered, and Lipp began tc choke the life out of him, while Jordison held down his'legs and arms. With a stupendous effort he freed one of his hands, and thrust it into his pocket. He had a confused idea that there was a weapon there. Quick as thought Jordison gripped his wrist, and as he inserted his hand into the pocket, the fingers touched the gold watch. He drew it out. Now Jordison knew that Jenny had no gold watch, and he gave a quick glance of curiosity at it, before flinging it aside. In that brief moment he caught sight of a name engraved on the back of the case, and for the time being he forgot everything else. He loosed Jermy, and slipping the watch into his pocket, sprung to his feet. " Let the man go, Lipp," he cried. " I want to speak to him. I can shoot him if he runs."

Lipp loosed the throat, but stood over the fellow like a cat watching a mouse. Jermy did not move. He was nearly black in the face, and struggling to get his breath. "Where did you get this watch ? " asked Jordison. 4 faint hope flickered in Jermy's breast. ' He noted the look on his master's face as he asked the question. Here was something that was required of him, and he knew that no answer could be got out of a dead man. He regained his wits—the wits of a professional scoundrel. I'll tell ye: if ye'll take me 'ome," he replied, gasping for breath. "and swear you won't try this game again." "If you'll swear you won't peach on us, and tell me what I want," Jordison replied, " give you my word of honour as a gentleman, that we will not harm you." Jermv swore the oath in picturesque language, and Jordison gave him the required assurance. Then Jordison and Lipp grasped the man by the arms and led him to the motor- car. In a few minutes they were in the Red House.

Then over a substantial supper Jermy told his comrades how he had found the gold watch, but he said nothing of the body in the mad jordison's face feV, and there was an ugly look on it, but he remembered his promise. He felt that he had been tricked, for the news was quite valueless. . .

Yet that night Cri his bedroom he turned the watch over and over in his hand, locking at it as though he expected it to speak to him. But it only spoke the five words engraved or the back of it "Arthur Stexims, from his mother."

world as that of one of the prisoners in the ola dungeons of Venice. Arthur Hohne resolved to deal with his heart in his own way. He knew the limita- tions of human nature, and decided to break off all communication with the people at Heatherstone Hall. His only home was in the fifteen ton boat that lay in the Essex creeks. He had no ties. There was no question of changing house or shifting furni- ture. He had but to set sail, and move into another part of the country. Savp for Lady Agnes, he cart not care a straw tor the ultra I.,ilisation of Heatblerstone Hall. 'His friend Lord Harry Quy, was up in town contributing 3. solid vote and golden silence to the conser- vative cause. press clothes and a footman at ais elbow did not appeal to Arthur Holme. He was only really happy when he was hanging on to- the tiller with his feet pressed against the bulwarks, and the frothy water was sweeping past him like a mill race.. To break aff all eOnnection with the Heatherstones would only mean the loss of Lady Agnes. And that would be all for her good. And so the young man resolved to fly from tempta- tion. It was no act of weakness, for a man must be super human to be long with the woman he loves and be silent. It was rather, from a man's point of view, an act of strength, sacrifice for the good of the woman. Yet it is pitiful to learn how small a thing will shatter all the resolutions of a brave man.

Arthur Holme strung his news on the Heatherstone' household at dinner one night. He longed, so he said, for a breath of the sea. He had work to do,. work that Lord Heatherstone dkl not , approve of, but still, afork that had. to be done. His heart was with the toilers on the deep, and he had resolved to take the " Rover ' to Yarmouth, and, if possible, adjust a dispute between the smack owners and the men, which threatened the livelihood of thousands.

Lord Heatherstone received his news with genuine regret. The other gifests murmured various commonplaces. One lady said that she had worked woollen comforters for the North Sea fishermen. Lady Agnes was I silent, but a close observer might have noted the tremor of her hand as she lifted a glass of I water to her lips. Arthur Holme did not I dare to look at her face. He felt the cruelty of this sudden and public announcement. But he had resolved that there should be no fare- well scene between them, and that the girl he loved should both receive the news of his I departure in the presence of her family, and Ibe finally included in a formal leavetaking. It was therefore the irony of fate that I brought these two together after they had I said good-bye in the presence of a dozen other people. A few minutes before mid- night Arthur Holme and Lord Heatherstone sat alone in the smoking-room. The guests had departed, and Lord and Lady Gambridge, who were staying. in the house, had gone to bed. The noble Earl was expounding his views on labour and capital, a subject intro- duced by Holme's projected visit to the scene of a great labour dispute. He quoted copiously from the speeches of men long dead, and from the works of authors who were deservedly forgotten. He was a man ol accurate memory for. unimportant details, and referred to fifty works that Holme had never heard of. In one case, however, his , memory failed him. It was necessary to refer I to the book. Holme protested that he would accept the quotation without reference. But Lord Heatherstone would have none of this. It would be better, he said, to refer to the book. He was not quite sure if he had the book, but he fancied it was in the library. it was in the house, it was in section H, shelf 7. Holme rose to his feet. "I will look for it, Lord Heatherstone," he said, perhaps a trifle wearily. It was getting late, and he did not care a rap for the authority in question. But he was anxious to humour a man from whom I he had received much kindness and whom he might never see again. As he made his way across the hall to the library he was surprised to see the door open, and noticed that there was a faint glow in the room, as though a single light had been switched on at the far end of it. It was an enor- mous apartment, nearly 200 feet in length. The bookshelves covered the walls to the height of eight feet from tha ground, and ran out in wings to the centre of the. room. He entered quietly, and his footsteps made no sound on the thick pile carpet. He knew that section H was half way , down the side opposite the door, and making Ihis way round one of the projecting walls of books, he walked down the centre aisle. As he did so, he heard a faint sound at the far end of the room, the sound of books being replaced in their shelves. He walked rapidly past section H and then paused, for another sound came to his ears, the heartbreaking sobs of a woman, low, I stifled, but distinct in the silence. An ex- pression of pain crossed his face, and he stood Irresolute, trembling in every limb. He knew well who it was that wept. And then in a single moment his whole 'being went out to the woman he was deserting, the woman who Loved him. Prudence, honour, the know- ledge of what was best for both of them, were all swept to the four winds of Heaven. His love and his pity, rode triumphant over every- thing. And yet for a moment he paused. He had purposely avoided a farewell scene. Here it lay, ready made to his hand! He was a strong man, but he paused only for a few seconds. In that time he prayed that the sobbing might cease. But it did not ceas,t, and the sound of it made him a poor weak thing. He clenched his hands in despair, and strode rapidly to the end of the room. He purposely made a noise before he came to the last wing of books, and when he passed it, Lady Agnes Cliffe was looking diligently at the shelves as though in search of something. She half turned towards him with a faint smile on her flushed face. •

To be with people in an hour like this counts for many days of ordinary intercourse, and so it came to pass that Arthur Holme became very intimate with the Heatherstone folk, and found 'his way into the heart of at least one person in the family. WOMEN'S CHAT.

• The Season at its Height. —The Queen'axeturn to 'London has given a new stimulus to social gaieties, and Derby week has witnessed an extraordinary rush of .-Society entertainments. Chief and foremost among ;these were the two Courts at Buckingham Palace, at which Her Majesty made her first appearance for the Season. It is most unusual for two such ceremonies to be held in one week, but it was found necessary to have •the Courts over—they being the last of the Season—in consequence of the:visit of the King of Spain and its attendant State entertainment, and the arrangements for the Royal wedding at Windsor the following, week. There will be little pause in the succession of Royal festivities until after Ascot, and that fashionable race meeting is to be made exceptionally brilliant this year. Aleut from the Royal ceremony at Windsor, there is no great Society wedding that stands out for particular mote this season, but fashionable marriages are. once in swing•now that the ill-omened month of May is vover, and these help •to swell the ceaseless round of receptions, dances, gaieties and entertainments, which the world of rank and fashion calls the London-Season. The Royal Family of Sweden.—The King of Sweden, who is now in his seventy-seventh year, has on former -occasions paid us several visits, and his grandson Pritce <instal- Adolph is not the first member of his family who has come to be married in this country. His second 'eon, Prince. Oscar, whilst staying at Bournemoutbin 1889 became engaged toldiss Menck, and their marriage took I place at that well-known watering place the same year, the Queen of Sweden being present. The Royal family of Sweden is the most democratic in all Europe, and *Prince Oscar's marriage with Miss Munck did not cause any troubles, though be, of colarse, resigned all claims to the right of succession, and assumed the title of Prince Bernardotte. That is the family name. The original Bernadotte, the grandfather of the present King of Sweden, was the son of a petty French attorney, but tbserose to be one of Napoleon's marshals, and was 'elected King of.-Sweden in succession to Charles-NIL. An Unfounded Attack on Royel Patriotism.—ln much the same way at the rhetorician sets up an "Aunt ally" in orderto shew how edectively it can he knocked to pieces, so a leading drapery journal affects -to be very angry because, it says, a great part of Princess Margaret's trees-seat-1 has been ordered abroad, and more. .over the present Royal custom of holding evening Courts, is very much worse for trade than the afternoon drawing room of Queen Victoria's time. It is quite 'true that for Princess Margaret's outfit a few pretty things have been ordered from Paris, but with trifling -exceptions, the whole of the orders have been distri- buted between British and Irish firms, and there is j nothing to justify tee assertion. that the Princess will be the first bride of the reigning house, to wear any but fßritish made wedding dress." There has been a 1 similar outcry in Berlin, respecting the trousseau of the Dnehess Cecile, but whatever may be tbe facts there, no .departure has been made in the case of Princess 1 Margaret from the Royal custom of favouring British! and Irish firms. Norie there any room to believe that the fashionable world spends less upon toilettes because evening Courts have taken the place of afternoon draw- ing rooms. The general opinion is quite to the con- trary, and seeing the more active part now taken by the Court at the bead of Society, it is absurd to suggest that it does less to ,encourage home trade than in the reign and long widowhood of our late'Queen. Fashions in Artificial Flowers.—Margearites and daisies should only be worn by quite young girls,so says I en authority on the subject, and in the list of suitable artificial flowers set apart fa: those still in their teens, are included 'brinksia roses, forget-me-nots, snowdrops, , lilies-of-the-yalley, and sweet pens. Violets,,,pansies, I chrysanthemums, mignonette, hyacinths, and tulips are enitable for the woman past the hey-day of her youth. White thistles are to be preferred for the elderly woman possessed of grey or white hair. With regard to natural flowers, the fashion is to select one particular 'bloom, and remain faithful to it, a fashion se,t tryiher Majesty the Queen, who is seldom if ever seen without a knot of lilles-of-the-valley. -Princess Henry of Pa9SS rrefers her name flower—the daisy, the ,Ducheias Portland invariably wears carnations, while Lady 1 Algernon Gordon-Lennox favours gardenias.

-- - The Royal Trousseau.—Art entire absence of exaggera- tion marks the trousseau dresses prepared for Princest lklargaret of Connaught. The skirts are all ample and flowing about the feet,but neither orinoline nor feather- bone has been resorted to, moreover the sleeves are but moderately full and quite unstiffenad. Pale pink, and pale blue, are the Princess's favourite colours, but the palest of greens is included in the colour schemes. Ireland and Buckinghamshire are supptying much of the lace, and many superb pie' oes show designs of her Royal Highness's name-flower. This in miniature, also appears upon all the lingerie, the greater patt of which has come from Ireland.

A Leading Color.—The craze forpale blue is no doubt correctly ascribed to the preference shewe by the future Crown Princess of Prussia for this particular hue. Unquestionably it is a very becoming mode, and the woman who restricts herself to pale blue and white this summer, will find the scheme a very delightful one, and I fairly economical. White is by no MOODS the extravagant wear it was once deemOd to be, and many girls and women who have to dress upon a decidedly slender allowance, are donning nothing else just now for day wear. White Irish homespun is idea Lit cleans splendidly and can even be washed without fear of shrinking or spoiling. Grand Opera Fashionable, but not Popular.—There is to he a gala opera performance at Covent Garden next week, in honour of the King of Spain. The young king is known to be very fond of horses and motors, a somewhat unusual combination'of tastes—but one has never heard that he is musical. That however, is not very material, as there are many patrons of grand opera —especially among the nouveau riehc, who have no 1 interest in music, hut who are regular attendants at the opera, because it is the thing to do. Onfa gaht night the wonderfnlfdisplay of jewels, and the gorgeously decorated house, more than over diverts attention from the stage to the auditorium, and on Thursday, when the court and the King of Spain are to be present the zpectocle will be a brilliant one indeed. On such occa. sions very few tickets are available for the general public, and for such as there are, anything from two to -fifteen guineas would have to be paid. People do not mind payirg such prices to see Royalty and the brilliant scene, though they would not pay as many shillings for the musical performance. Grand Opera in foreign languages and by foreign artistes has in fact little (arm" attraction for the general British public,and though a fresh attempt is being made at one of the new houses in London to provide such an entertainment at moderate prices, it remains to be seen whether it will prove itifferent from the many failures which have pro- ceeded it.

PRINCESS IVIARGARET'S WEDDING-DRESS.

Princess Margaret of Connaught is, says Vanity Fair, the first English Princess who has had her wedding-dress made in Pails. But the lace with which it is entirely covered, and is by far the most costly part of the garment, is of Irish manufacture, as is the veil which the Royal bride will wear over her wreath of myrtle and orange blossom.

SCEXDISIL GIRLS THS BEST. •

The best and most highly-trained servant I eves 'net, says a writer in the Daily Dispatch, was a Swedish girl. Her knowledge of household work and management was a marvel to everyone. Nothing came amiss to her. At cooking, laundry work, dressmaking, or housemaid's duties she was equally at home and efficient, and all her duties were performed with the deftness of a practised hand. I asked her how and where she had gained her knowledge. She answered: "At the household school"; and then went on to explain tbat in Sweden there were regular schools at which girls could attain proficiency in every department of household work. The course embraced cooking, laundry work, weaving, dressmaking, general housework, and gardeninz. The students had lessons in management, and were taught the value of money by being sent out marketing. At the end of the course certificates were granted to t nse who passed an examination. -ernes° eertificatei were highly prized, and their holders were much sought alter as dornestic servants. Since then I have often thought that the only practical and effectual solution of the servant problem lies in the opening of household schools in every large town and city.

New designs in lingerie parasols tonow each other in quick succession. One simple and charm- ing model carried Out in fine white linen has a bunch of heavily embroidered violets on each, panel. This is caught with a large bow of open- work embroidery. Embroidery in white or self tone is considered smarter than that of a contrast- ing colour, parasols of ecru lawn embroidered irs white being, however, an exception to this rule, as they are particularly effective.

The World has some piquant gossip from Berlin about what it describes • as the Imperial mita. !lance. The approaching marriage of the Kaiser's eldest son, it says, is regarded with the most complete indifference by the overwhelming majority of Germans—nay, by practically the whole German Illation. In society the marriAtre is regarded with 'narked disapproval as a isisalliaqty in every sense of the word. The ladies of the great feudal houses are indignant at the thought of a little Mecklen- burg duchess, a mere highness, being placed over their heads—and with such a, mother, too! Then they say the Kaiser himself is nut too keen on the union. The marriage means, of course, great expense, and the Kaiser is always more or less short of money-. Besides, Part 'Petre grandere is not one that he has any wish to cultivate just at present. As for 'the K.*erin, she does no attemi t to conceal her regret that the Crown Prince should get married at all. tie is her favourite son. The mere thought of another woman, no matter whom, being dearer than herself to any of her male folk, especially the Kaiser or the Crown Prince, is almost more than she can hear.

CALVES' SWEETBREAD SALAD.—CICRD two sweet- breads, parboil them, and let them stAnil in cold water for thirty-five minutes.. Trim them neatly, and simmer gently for half-an-hour, adding a pinch of salt and pepper. Remove and set on one side to cool. When cold cut the sweetbre.ids into tine slices. Make a good mayonnaise sauce, drop the slices of sweetbread into it, mix well,and servo In the centre of a salad-bowl, surrounded by lettuce, mustard-and-cress. and Boring leeks.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

THE BLACA MOTOR CAR.

By .7. B. HARRIS BURLAND.

'CHAPTER XlV.—Conttnueb

'With hands and arms unbound- the rest was

easy. He cut through all the ropes in five minutes and, rising to his feet, moved pain- fully-across the room to a cupboard, where he found a bottle of brandy. A deep draught of the fiery liquid put fresh life into kis aching limbs and body. He went hurriedly to a dirty old cigar box full of papers and drew out a purse with twenty-five sovereigns in it. 'Then he glanced at the window and door, decided on the latter,' and burst it ,open with the heavy iron fender. Then he made his way downstairs, crammed half a loaf and a chunk of bacon into his pocket and slipped out into the road. There was no one in sight, but he could not see far in any direction. A white mist was rising from the marshes, and the road dis- appeared from view three hundred yards from the Red House. The sun had set, but the sky was clear, and not yet dark. The full moon showed large and yellow on the horizon through a bank of sea fog. Jermy stood a moment at the gate and listened. The silence was complete and unbroken. He wondered what lay beyond the wall of mist;, whether jordison and Lipp were tearing at full speed 'to the nearest seaport, or whether Susanson had been captured. The motor was so silent that it might flash upon him at any moment out of the mist. Any other machine would have given warning of its approach, and the low throb of the engines would have been heard two miles away. But this black monster moved like a ghost, and till its lights flashed suddenly out of the dark- ness, no man could know how near it Nag •to him.

Jermy had, however, to take his chance, and he set off along the road to Gorehaven. It was the only route open to him. In all directions he was cut off by winding creeks, full almost to the banks, for a Spring tide was running up from the sea, and it was nearly high water.

On either side of the road lay two tall banks; beyond them were two strips of marsh land, intersected by a thousand tiny channels and gullies.. At low tide these were merely narrow ravines of mud, some more than six feet in depth, and some but shallow little gutters, tapering off into a point, losing them- selves in thick masses of purple sea lavender. But on this night they were full to the brim with water.

Jenny decided that the open road was too perilous for his purpose, so he climbed a steep bank on the left and descended to the marsh land on the other side of it. A rude pathway ran at the foot. In some places it was over- grown with glasswort,, now turning to a mass of crimson spears, in others it was a narrow stretch of mud littered with corks, bottles, scraps of wood and sticks, reeds, and a thou- sand and one pieces of debris that had drifted up the creek with the tide. The bank was over six feet high, and he was well screened from observation, but the walking was difficult, and h; moved slowly on his journey. Every now and then he crawled cautiously tc the top of the.bank, and peering over the edge, looked up and down the road. . . _

A faint wind had risen from the south-west. and the mist was gradually being scattered before it. In half an hour's time he could see nearly a mile in every direction. At the end of an hour he had travelled little more than three miles, but his limbs were still stiff from the ropes that had cut into his flesh and muscles, and he was beginning to be tired of his journey. He lay down on the bank to rest, so that his eyes could scan the long road beyond. The bank was wet with a heavy dew and so steep that he stood almost upright as he leant against. it. But it afforded a •certain degree of comfort after his weary tramp on the slippery and overgrown path. He pulled out the bread and bacon and ate heart- ily; then he tilled a short clay pipe with black shag and smoked till a sense of peace crept over him, and he saw himself the owner of

5,000. He had no doubt that the stupid little Susanson was dead.

His happy meditations were ihterrupted by a yellow glow in the mist, where the road vanished from sight. A few seconds later three bright sparks glittered in the distance like stars, and every second they increased in till he could see the black car behind: chem. In less than a minute they had flashed past him, and the fan of light receded farther and farther till it died away in the mist beyond.

He sprang to .his feet and continued his journey. He had noted that there were only two people in the car. It was quite evident that Susanson had been effectually disposed of, for it was hardly likely that the two men would have returned to the Red House: if the little Jew had made good his escape. The reward was in Jermy's hands, if he could only get off the inarshla.nd into the open country._ _

At present, he was .so bound up among the ,creeks and inlets on either side of the road, that he could only escape by hiding. He knew well enough that before low' the motor ,would come tearing back along the 'road, and that the occupants would organise a system- atic search on either side of it.

He sprang to his feet and Continued his journey. lie had nearly three miles to go before he could take to the fields, and strike Across country to Gorehaven. He had a feW ,miles start, but this, even with the time that They would occupy in discovering hi:, absence, twis.not much for a man in a race with a motor that could run itoo miles in tbe'llour.

The wind had cleared, away the last rem- nant of the fog, and the full moon shone clear over the country. The marsh land was :richly veined with silver where the light fell on the thousand creeks and gullies. It was a singuUrly beautiful sight, but Jermy cursed it with a foul mouth. He would have liked a fog so dense that a map could not see his own feet.. In ten Ininute:3 dine he peeped over the edge of Ow bank, and again saw three bright eyes in the 4istance, and a blotch of black on the long white road. He hastened on his way, bending down a little in case they should catch some glimpse -of him from the passing car.

When at last he heard the swish of mud, he lay fiat on the ground amonn, some tall marsh weed. The car passed and h rose to his feet. He knew well enough the method of his pur- suers. They would go to the end of the marshes where the road joined the old Roman causeway, and then they would beat back- wards oh foot, one on either side of the road. In the bright moonlight it would scarcely be possible for him to escape their notice. A pair of night glasses , would detect a moving figure figure on that flat surface for miles around. He stopped and hesitated whether to go forward or return. If he proceeded on his way,he would be bound ,to meet them. If he went back, he would perhaps escape them for the moment, but he was only running further into a trap, and • further from safety. If he had ibeen armed, he would have stood his ground and fought, but Jordison had taken good care that there should be only one re- volver at the Red House.

He finally decided to go back. He crept as near to the hank as he could, and in a few minutes crossed it, and kept close to it on the other side. If his estimate of his pursuers were correct, he would be hidden from their view. _ _

• Tor a quarter of an hour he trudged home- wards. Every now and then he turned back and saw the distant twinkle of the Bleriot lamps, motionless at the far end of the road. Then, as he looked, they seemed larger, and a minute later he realised that the motor was moving rapidly towards him. He saw now what had happened. He was discovered. Ile crawled over the edge ot the bank again, and leant against it, sweating at every pore. Once, twice, three times he saw a figure stand on the edge of the bank, and each time nearer

THE ALLEGED RAILWAY EMBEZZLEMENT.

Unexpected Development.

WARRINGTON C.L.C. PORTERS ACQUITTED.

On Tuesday at the Liverpool City Sessions, John Thomas Hudson (40), railway porter, was charged with having at Warrington on the 24th April, embezzled 4s. od. and with having on the Bth May, embezzled as., the moneys having been received by him on account of the Cheshire Lines Committee. Mr. Rigby Sidift and Mr. H. L. Riley (instructed by Messrs. A. Browne and C 0.,) prosecuted and prisoner, who pleaded not guilty, was defended by Mr. Madden and Mr. Cuthbert Smith,(instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel). Mr. Swift stated that prisoner had occupied the position of foreman porter at Warrington Station, on the Cheshire Line's Committee's system. On alternate weeks he was on night duty at the station, being then practically the stationmaster in charge. Each morning three trains left Warrington for Liverpool at 4, 4.30, and 5.35. The booldnF office was not open at this early hour, but the booking-clerk, before going off duty, left with the prisoner a. packet of tickets which he sold to passengers. The tickets were afterwards collected, either by the prisoner or by one of his subordinates. The case against the prisoner was that he sold the tickets and got the money. The passenger then got into the train, and his ticket was collected and handed to the prisoner, who, however, instead of punching it, and so cancelling it, put it back again into the packet,as though itthad never been used at all, pocketing the money. Private detectives were engaged, and the alleged frauds were discovered.

A number of witnesses were called to give evidence in corroboration.

A young lady deposed to having travelled from War- rington to Liverpool,and having received a ticket which had been sold to another passenger early in the morning and which had not been cancelled after beigg collected. Philip Darbyshire, stationmaster at Warrington for the past nine years, was cross-examined by Mr. Madden as to the duties of the booking clerks and other em- ployes at the station. He said a booking clerk came on duty at 5.40 every morning. Mr. Madden: Do you know that the booking clerk comes on duty at 5.40 on Monday and not on the other days of the week .li7Witness..:. I don't know. . .

bees he negleetihis duty if he is not there for the 5.40 P—Yes.

Do you know the tickets on the other mornings are issued frequently by a telegraph clerk ?—I was not aware of that. It should not be done. Do you know the clerks are in the habit of coming to their duties as late as seven and eight in the morning? —lnm not aware of that.

Mr. Madden: lam afraid there are a good many things you are not aware of on the station. Mr. Swift: That is no reason why they should be robbed.

Mr. Madden: Do you know that on the other morn- ings except Monday the booking clerk passes the job over to the telegraph clerk ?—Witness: I don't know that.

Further questioned, Mr. Darbvshire said there were rules as to collecting tickets. The tickets were some- times collected by porters and placed on the table in the collectors' room to be cancelled by the collectors. The porter did not cancel them. It was not a fact that tickets were left for hours on the table without being snapped. 'Mr. Madden: Can you swear there are rules of that kind ?—Witness : Yes, in print and in writing. The Chairman read a copy of the instructions which had been handea in, stating that :—`• The foreman will be held responsible for cancelling and examining tickets when the collector is not on duty, and the collector when on duty." "Surely that is enough," added Sir William ForwoOd. "Those are instructions dated 1901. I don't think it is necessary to go beyond that." Mr. Madden: The conduct of this case goes back a long time. Mr. Derbyshire further explained that on a Sunday night the tickets for sale were banded to the police officer on duty at the station to give to the foreman porter when he started work. Mr. Madden : Have you asked your superiors for this to be done ?—Witness : No. It was done before my time. He added that prisoner had been in the employ of the company for 25 years, for which period witness had known him. He (Mr. Darbyshire) had thought highly of him, and he had possessed witness's confidence. Mr. Swift: Have you ever heard before to-day that the money taken from the early morning trains has been handed to the telegraph clerk to give the booking clerk ?—Never.

Supposing the booking clerks were late in coming on duty, whose business was it to call attention to it? —The foreman, Hudson. He was responsible for the station until you got there ?—Yes.

If these irregularities had been occuring Hudson is responsible and not you ?—Yes. You have been asked whether Mr. Meldrum knew about the system. At any rate Hudson understood the System ?—Yes, perfectly. Has Hudson complained to you that there was any difficulty or impropriety in the way tickets were col- lected ?—No.

He has been doing it for four years ?—Yes. Mr. Madden: Don't you know Hudson had complained to Mr. Davies the chief clerk, about the system ?—No, sir ; I don't. A perter-guard named Henshall, stationed at War- rington, gave evidence. Mr. Madden: Even you said that sometimes you cancelled tickets when the foreman was busy?— No. Did you ever see the telegraph clerk issue tickets?— A time or two I have seen Hudson's son issue them.

Why have they issued tickets?—Because the booking clerk was not there. • .

Mr. Swift, addressing the Chairman, said the !question was whether Hudson had stolen 4s. 6d. or 3s. The cross-examination seemed to be directed to the whole of the management of the Cheshire Lines Station at IVarrington, and the duty of every particulzr clerk, and be submitted it was quite irrelevant. The Chairman: I think Mr. Madden is wide of the mark.

_ Mr. Madden : I may travel a little wide of the mark, but I know the point as well as my friend, and it is part of the issue to prove the Igeneral carelessness of the place. We are not going to be punished for the care- lessness of the management. The Chairman: e are not hear to try the manage- ment. It is no use wasting time. Mr. Madden : I shall not pursue it further after this witness.

Jack Beddard, a booking cleric, admitted, in reply to Mr. Madden, that on the Manchester side of the station it was the custom, except on Monday mornings, for the ticket money collected from the early morning trains to be left with the telegraph clerk who was on duty all night. This arrangement enabled the booking clerk to turn up later in the morning than would otherwise be necessary, and witness, although be admitted having I been reprimanded by the stationmaster some time ago, said he did not think he (the stationmaster) was aware of the existing arrangement. After an adjournment, Mr. Swift said he had had a conference with counsel for the defence, and also with the solicitor for the prosecution, and, having heard that the 'defendant* were prepared to admit that gross irregularities had taken niece in the receipt of money

and the non-payment of the same to the proper booking ollice clerk but that there was no fraudulent intent, he (Mr. Swift) had taken the matter into his own hands He had decided to offer no further evidence for the. prosecution, especially in view of the fact that the prisoner did not impute any base motives to any other official at the station, either superior or inferior. Mr. Maddon pointed to the exceedingly good char- acter that his client had held during the twenty-five years he had been in the committee's employ. He had' been in a position of trust,and he accepted the responsi- bility and was sorry for the irregularities which had , occurred whilst he was in charge. Ile had no fraudulent intent, and he would be sorry to make any reflections upon anyone else in the station. The Chairman, addressing the jury, said there was no doubt that there had been gross irregularities, and the absence'of system at the station had no doubt led to these irregularities. After the statement of Mr. Swift, however, the jury had no option but to find a verdict of " Not guilty." The jury thereupon returned a verdict of "Not guilty" The Chairman, in discharging the prisoner, said he was a very fortunate fellow. He must thank the counsel for the prosecution for the course he had taken. The irregu- larities had been very serious, and prisoner had escaped by a technicality. He hoped it would be a warning to him for the future. The Prisoner : Thank you, sir. ANOTHER ACQUITTAL. Edward Hughes (52), railway porter at Warrington Station, was next called into the dock on a similar charge. _

MK Swift said the explanation in this case was the same as in the last—that the prisoner bad been irregular and careless in the handling of the money,but that there was no fraudulent intent, He had been for thirty years in the employ of the Cheshire Lines Committee, but the Committee,having discovered the irregularities,felt that an example should be made. No one was more pleased than the Cheshire Lines Committee in being able to believe that the men had no fraudulent intent, and that the worse that could be said of thera,after hearing their explanation, was that they had been pareless. Mr. Madden, for the defence, remarked that prisoner had worked long and loyally for the employers, whom he had had no intention of defrauding.

, THE BENCH AND THE PROSECUTION. The jury having returned a formal verdict of "Not guilty," The Chairmen said the prisoner would be discharged. He added that the bench thought the railway company bad exercised extraordinary leniency,in the course they had taken. They, however, accepted the statement of counsel that he was not able to carry the case any further, but the bench themselves did not feel quite satisfied. Mr. Swift said he wished the bench had intimated their view at an earlier stage, because before he expressed his willingness to accept a verdict of " Not guilty " ho endeavoured to make it plain that he would do nothing except with the approval of the bench•and he thought that the bench had approved of his action. The Chairman: We did not know you intended to put it in that way. Mr. Swift: I intended that, and I hope the bench will not.think that I have conducted this case as I ought not to have done. The Chairman: Oh, far from that. Mr. • Madden pointed out that nobody had yet heard the defence, and it was all subject to that. The Chairman: All subject to that, of course. M. Swift added that he had heard the nature of the defence from Mr. Madden—that there had been no felonious intent; and, having regard to the good character of the men, be thought that the jury might properly take the view that they ought not to be con- victed of embezzlement.

Where did you come from, baby dear? Out of the everywhere into -nere. Where did you get your eyes so blue? Out of the sky as I canie through. What makes the light in them sparkle and spin? Some or the starry joy left in. Where did you get that 'little tear/ I found it waiting when I gut here. What makes your forehead so smooth and high? A soft hand stroked it as I went by. What makes your cheek like a warm, white

rose I saw something better than 'anyone knows. Whence that three-cornered smile of bliss? Three angels gave me at once—a kiss. From the same :box. as cherubs' wings. How did they all just come to be you? - God thought of me, and so I grew. And how did you come to us, you dear? God thought of you, and so I'm here. CHARACTER TELLS!

Some years ago, in a farming neighbourhood, a middle-aged man was looking about in search of employment. He called at the house of a respectable farmer and told him his errand.

"What is you name?" asked the farmer. "John Wilson," was the reply. "John Wilson—the same that lived here when a boy?" "The same, sir." "Then I don't want you." Poor John, surprised at such a reply, passed on to the house of the next fanner, and there a similar reply was given. And be found no one in the neighbourhood where his earlier years had been spent who was willing to em- ploy him. Passing on he soon came in siglit of the school-house. "Ah," said he, "I understand it now. 1 was a school-boy there years ago; but what kind of a school-boy? Lazy, disobedient, often in mischief, and once caught in deliberate lying; and, though since I have been trying to reform, they all think me the same kind of a man that I was as a boy. Oh, that I had done as I ought when at school; then people would have confidence in me now."

So it is; and school-boys and school-girls should remember it, that character follows us and is remembered, and that those who have known us in our early days will be very apt to look upon ns in later years as they did in our youth. A lazy boy generally makes a lazy man, just as a crooked sapling makes a crooked tree. And so a shiftless, careless, mis- chievous, untruthful boy is likely to have the same character as he grows up to manhood. Even if he has changed, it is hard to make people believe it; for, as someone has said, If the crack has been mended, people will always be looking where it was," and, as the father told his son :—" You may draw out the nails you have driven, but the holes in th• post will remain!"

The Examiner, Saturday, June 3, 1905.

176 101 115 88 BEAUTIFUL ENGLISH PEERESS Praises Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People.

Though sharing the traditional reluctance a our old nobility to emerge from aristocratic privacy and come before the public as the subject of a newspaper artrcle, the Right Hon. Lady Haldon lies exoressly permitted the publication of a statement recently made ton repre- sentative of the Press regarding her wonderful care by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. Recognising that the words or a titled lady in her position must necessarily bear great weight with the

iohnston and Hoffmann.)

public, and earnestly desiring that the benefits she had derived from Dr. Williams' Pink Pills shonld be publicly acknowledged, Lady Heiden waived all personal reluct- ance and told a story which cannot but impress every reader. " For the sake of all sufferers," said her ladyship, "I want to tell you what Dr. Williams' 9in'r Pills for Pale People did for me when I had given up all hope, after the best professional treatment. " 1 have been a great traveller. Five veers ago,when residing in Austmlia,my heart became seriously affected. Symptoms of advanced Anaemia showed themselves. One of the results was most acute Indigestion. I suffered severely in the back and under the shoulder blades, in- cludingan especially severe oppression of the chest. The most careful diet brought no relief. Even after a glass of water my discomfort was almost unbearable. Food of any kind caused 1113 such misery that I ate less and less, till I was practically starving myself. In walking the shortest distances palpitations of the heart would make me feel as though I was suffocating. "As aumraia developed I grew alarmingly ill ; I conld not sleep at night; my whole system was deranged. My nerves broke down; my head ached constantly, until my eyesight became affected. "My illness seemed so deeply rooted that I deter- mined to go to the Clinique de Is Caroline, in Switzer- , land, where I was attended by eminent physicians. "But about August, 1903, while I was travelling by train and nearly prostrate with one of my attacks, I casually picked up a small pamphlet referring to Dr. Williams Pink Pills for Pale People. I became so interested in some cures mentioned therein that I determined to give a trial to this medicine. "Within three days I was astonished at the marvel- lous improvement. I procured further boxes of the pills, and very soon I was delighted to fled that all traces of my complaint had practically vanished. "I could now walk long distances with an entire free- dom from pain. My appetite had returned with a hearti- ness that surprised rue, and my general health was such 161 90 as I had not enjoyed for many years." OUR LOCAL, CHURCHES.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. The usual meeting of this society was held on Sunday last when there was a fair attendance. Mr. William Beswick, sen., occupied the chair. His subject of address was "Praising God in song, for his bounties." Mr. Williamson of Manchester was the speaker his snbject being "The wastes of life, financially, physically and morally." He was listened to with great interest by the members. Mr. Butcher rendered two violin solos in a very pleasing manner, Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., accompanying on the organ. One new member received the usual hearty welcome into the Brotherhood.

Special Services at Moore. On Sunday the Sunday school anniversary services were held at the Moore • ‘Vesleyan Church, when there were very large congregations. The preacher was the Ray. W. C. J. Neville, of Derby, formerly offfarrington. At the afternoon service he emphasized very impressively the responsibilities of parents and teachers The evening sermon was founded on St. John iv. 24, and succeeded in maintaining the close attention of the largo gathering of hearers, as the spirituality of true worship was discussed. The singing, as usual, was an impressive feature of the services, the choir being con- ducted by Mr. Levi Richardson, while Mr. John Banner presided at the organ. The anthems ‘vere well rendered. Some verses of the hymns were given as quartets by Mrs. Birch, Messrs. James Antrobus, Joseph Roylance and John Cartwright, while one hymn was sung as a solo by a junior scholar Miss Alice Lawton.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
Open Air Services at Hill Cliffe.

The first open air services for the season in csonnec- don with the Baptist Church was held on Sunday evert- ing on the steps, and on the open space of grouud on the • side of the lane, the congregation overflowing a little into the lane. Owing to the favourable character of the weather a commencement has been made a fortnight earlier than last year. The weather on Sunday evening was quite ideal for the purpose, and a fairly good con- gregation assembled. The service was conducted by the Pastor, the Hey. J. S. Hughes, who spoke on "The general attitude towards religion—is It reasonable?" Hymn papers were provided and well-known hymns were sung with heartiness and vigour. The compact congregation, gathered by the side of the old chapel, under tne shadow of the old sycamore in full leaf, was a pretty sight. The preacher stood beside the lectern on the higher steps and the organ, presided over by Miss Jones, was placed on the lower steps.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
Christ Church Padgate.

At Christ Church, Padgate, on Sunday the annual sermons on behalf of the day and Sunday schools of Christ Church, Padgate, were preached. The morning preacher was the Rev. G. C. Briggs, of Christ Church, Latchford, and the evening preacher the Rev. T. R. Matthews, vicar of Earlestown. Both gave a review of the controversy regarding religious instruction in day schools, and pleaded with their hearers to insist on definite religious teaching forming a part of the scrip- tural lessons given to children. The musical portion of the services was well rendered by the choir, the anthem sung at the morning service, 'Tor His name is excellent," going particularly well. In the evening the anthem, "They shall mount up with wings as eagles " (Simper) was sung. The school managers are at present negotiating for the purchase of a plot of land behind the school in order to provide a playground, and thus remove the children from the road, with its ever-increasing danger from passing traffic. I This, with the, fencing, will cost about £lOO, while it is contemplated that the necessary outbuildings will, cost :a similar suna. In addition to this, his Nlajesay's inspec- tor has, through the Education Committee, drawn the I managers attention to the necessity of dividing up the Large room by movable partitions. This will prove a costly matter. The offertories amounted to £3 in excess of last year.

THE INFIRMARY. Mrs. A. M. BERESFORD, lady guardian for Derby, who visited the house last Thursday wrote :--I wpnld like to express my pleasure in going over the new Infirmary, and also mention the kindness of the matron in showing me all the new improvements in the Infirmary. I was very pleased with the cleanliness and order everywhere, and the personal interest the matron takes in all the inmates.

TILE HOUSE.

The MASTER reported that the number of inmates in the House on ‘Vedneaday morning was 476 as compared' with 493 for the corresponding week last year. During the weak 310 vagrants .had been relieved, men and children, compared with 302 for the corresponding week of last year.

_PROPOSED, SALARY INCREASES.

At a meeting of the Cottage Homes' Committee on May 30th, it was resolved that the salaries of the superintendent and matron be each increased £5 yearly also that Miss Dykes' salary be increased £2 10s. yearly. The application of the other foster mothers was deferred.

It was pointed out that tho water could be fully discussed at the nett Board meeting, if necessary, as the increase would not take effect until the expiration of the question. The minutes were confirmed. THE A CCONINIOII ATION QUESTION.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED UTILIZATION_OF THE OLD INFIRMARY.

At a meeting of the House Committee held on the 26th May it was reported that the sub-committee oppointed to deal with Mr. Hickman'c resolution with regard to the proposed utilization of the old Infirmary ,to provide additional accommodation had appointed Messrs. Owen as professional advisers. The result of the committee's deliberations was submitted as follows :—" Messrs. Owen having submitted sketch plans and explained certain matters which emerged during their consideration it was resolved that it is desirable, firstly that the whole of the accommo- dation on the gro,ind floor of the female side of the old hospital be allocated for the use of female lunatics and imbeciles; secondly that the ground floor of the male side including the present word+ shops, be allocated for the use of infirm men; thirdly, that new workshops be erected 20ft, wide, as indicated on the plan now submitted,and as alternatives, to be one or two storeys in height ; also that Messrs. Owen be empowered to approach the Local Government Board with a view of ascertaining whether these proposed • arrangements would he approved by them with the pur- pose, if this were probable, of the subsequent prepara- tion of full plans, working,. drawings, and estimates." The recommendations were adopted Ananimously. Mr. FORSILLW moved the adoption of the minutes, and said he thonght the Board would be satisfied with the Committee's decision. As soon us the Committte were prepared with a full report, they would submit it to the Board, • The minutes ware approved.

UNDER-FEB SCHOOL CHILDREN.

How the New Order is to be Worked

in Warrington.

As we recently announced, the provision of meals to I underfed school children has, under a Local Government I Board Order to be undertaken by Board of Guardians who will deal with cases brought, before their notice by the local Education Authorities. Arrangements are being made to put the Order into operation at Warring- ton, and at the meeting of the Board of Guardians on Wednesday the Clerk (gr. J. C, Sutton), read the following letter from Mr. J. Moore Murray, Director of Education :—I beg to inform you that at the last meeting of the Education Committee the ciicillar from the Board of Education with reference to the relief of underfed children was under consideration. The Committee appointed me their officer to make special applications in respect of such children and I should be glad if you would inform me whether you have any suggestions to make as to the method of pro- cedure.

The CLERK said he thought it best to wait upon Mr. Murray and consider the subject with him. So far as his own experience went, from reading Poor Law publications and the Press generally, he did not see that the Order had been put into absolute operation • any- where. His judgment was that the issue of the Order had had a good effect upon the people who did not want to risk disfranchisement or to be exposed to the criti- cism of the public if they did not feed their children. In the event of applications being received it would be necessary to go to the relieving otficer for the particular district in which the children lived, or some special arrangement would have to be made if the work became considerable. Per the present,seeing that Mr. Shearer would in all cases have to collect the money from the parents, as the Board's collector, and that he also filled the position of assistant Telieving officer, if the applications were sent to him in the first instance they could be dealt with. The application of course, must come through the Education authorities' officers to Mr. Murray who would report them to an officer of the Union. At present he had not received any application from Mr. Murray, neither had Mr. Shearer. If any application came, Mr. Shearer would deal with it and report it to the Board.

WONDERFUL CURE Of SORE HANDS

After Most Awful Suffering Ever Ex— perienced Eight Doctors and Many Remedies Failed to Do a Cent's Worth of Good.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS TO CUTICURA IT IS ALL OVER NOW

"I was troubled with sore hands, so sore that when I would put them in water the pain would nearly set me crazy, the skin would peel off and the flesh get hard and break, with blood flowing from at least fifty places on each hand. Words could never tell the suf- fering I endured for three years. I tried eight different doctors, but none did me any good, as my hands were as bad as when I began.. I also tried many reme- dies, but none of them ever did me one cent's worth-of good. I was discouraged. and heart-sore. I would feel so bad mornings, to think I had to go to work. and stand the pain for ten hours' I often felt like giving up my position. I. would have to wrap every finger up separately, so as to try and keep them soft, and then wear gloves over the rags to keep the grease from getting on my work. I had to wear gloves at night; in fact, all the time. But thanks to Cuticura, that is all over now. After doctoring for three years, and spending much money, a single box of Cuticura Ointment ended all my sufferings. It's been two years since I, used any, and I don't know what sore hands are now, and never lost a day's work while using Cuticura. —Thomas A. Clancy, 310 North Montgomery St., Trenton, N. J."

A victory so overwhelm- THE FUTURE IN ing as that which the THE FAR EAST. Japanese Fleet has scored

- over its Russian foe takes some little time to appreciate at its full significance The details which .ire now coming in all go to prove both the magnitude and completeness of Admiral TOGO'S triumph. It is, indeed, not any exaggeration to say that the Russian Navy is now no more. The sea power of the CZAR is as com- pletely wiped out as was the sea power of NAPOLEON in 1805. ROJECESTVENSKI and his two principal Admirals are prisoners of war. All the battle squadron of the great Baltic armada has been sunk with the exception of two ships which have bean captured. An almost bewildering record to be sure. If matters go on as they have progressed during the eighteen months that the war has been in progress, we shall soon reach the limit of the stereotyped speculation. "What next ? " There will be no " next " left. One Armageddon was enough in the days of old. No nation can stand a succession of such cataclysms. The story of the fight is extraordinary. There are tales of mutiny, of sailors throwing themselves into the sea, and of officers who would have been brave enough on shore, but were paralysed on shipboard in the presence of the skilful and daring enemy. All this accumulated evidence of impotence has for the first time struck the Allies of Russia with dismay. Hitherto the French Press has mini- mised the Russian misfortunes, but now it has dawned on our neighbours that the whole business is irretrievable. With one voice public opinion thus calls on trio CZAR to make peace. Thia may no more than embitter the irritation of the Russian bureaucracy, already incensed by the refusal of France to grant another loan. The officials of St. Petersburg are very free with the assertion that the CZAR will persevere with the war, but ho he is to do it and maintain the financial credit of his country nobody has the least idea. Baron SIIYEMATSII remarked yesterday that "the bureau- crats must see there was no chance of improving their position,which possibly might come out worse if they continued the struggle." The Japanese Statesman added, with firm nut courteous irony, that "the Russians must give up all idea of regarding their own face as bigger than that of Japan." This, however, appears to be still the dominant idea at St. Petersburg. What Russia has to give up is her cherished supremacy in the Far East. She has to give it up as far as one can see at least for a generation. She will have to ' leave off meddling with China, for Japan will nexa have a possible word to say on the relations of , China to the rest of the world.

This aspeot of the case must be eminently dis- agreeable at Berlin, where the naval experts are very loth to believe in the Russian overthrow. All the KAISER'S beautiful projects for extending German territory at the expense of China will look uncommonly small when Japan imposes a naval and military veto. It is in the last degree improb- able that Japan will threatei, either the German or French positions in the Far East as they stand at present, but she will make it impossible to

increase them. Germany cannot send an army to China, and the Japanese Navy will soon be a matck for any Navy afloat except our own. All the European Powers will have to reconsider their relations with China in the light of the new situation. There is no "Yellow Peril" except to such ambition as Russia has fostered to her own undoing. China is not a I military State, and the idea of hordes of Chinamen over-running Europe is ludicrous when we see the majority of the Chinese unable to defend their own country against aggression. But it is quite conceivable that, if ever Japan and China should adopt a common policy, they will put an end to speculation. As we are not seeking any territory in the Far East this very rational compact would not affect us. Nor is it, easy to see how any British interests will be injured by the rise of Japan as a great Asiatic Power.

The annual report and statement of accounts for the Electricity Department is in many respects gratifying. A considerable increase of business during the year is recorded, while a net profit of £1,543 12s. 9d. is shown. It would have been better, however, if we could have had a similar statement from the Tramways D4artnient,for after all, the two are one in the eyes of the public. The bald announcement is published in the minutes of 'the committee at a profit of £4.5085. 6d. has been ' made on the tramways, thus reducing last years' deficit of £692 7s. 7d. to £:241 19s. id. These figures are, however, not very instructive. It will probably be a considerable number of years before either department is able to hand over any of the profits shown on the balance sheets for the relief of the rates, and until they are in this happy posi- tion we fear the public will refuse to look upon the Electticity Supply Undertaking and the Tram- ways as paying concerns. Both departments make a practice of ignoring depreciation in their state- statement of accounts, preferring to maintain plant out of revenue, and so long as this practice is adhered to there can be little hope of financial assistance from them. The profits which are declared, seem to us more illusory than real, and we should like to see a sounder system of finance adopted.

What may be termed the boating season at War- rington has opened with a very distressing fatality, no less than three lives being lost by the capsizing of a small skiff. Exactly how the accident happened will never be known for the three persons drowned were the only occupants of the boat,and no one on the banks of the river witnessed the over-turning of the boat. It is presumed, and with substantial reason, that the men were chang- ing seats, which is one of those reckless and frequently fatal things with no amount of warning will persuade people to avoid. There is not much boating at Warrington, but at Chester where there are hundreds of boats, one or two fatalities occur every year and usually from the same cause. In the height of the season one cannot row a mile or two up the river without see- ing some foolhardy persons changing seats in mid- stream, and usually cheerfully oblivious of the grest danger so simple an operation involves. We really think that it would be a wise thing for the Corp•irations of Chester and other pepular boating centres to make a bye-law rendering hirers of light craft liable to prosecution for thus recklessly endangering human life. There are plenty of police patrol boats about to ensure the bye-law being obeyed. If people revire to change seats they should pull to the side before doing so and thus minimise the danger. The Warrington fatality could doubtless have been avoided, and while we hope it will be a lesson to those who seek pleasure on the water, we add our deep sympathy to that of the relatives and friends of the two map and a boy who so suddenly and sadly lost their lives on Saturday.

The deliberations of the sub-committee appointed by the Council on the 9th November to consider and report upon Standing Order 45, relating to the wages clause in Corporation contracts havn been prolonged, but they do not carry matters very much further. Yet in our opinion they have resulted in the framing of a standing order which is explicit and perfectly fair. Substantially it insists on the observance of the standard rate of wages and hours of labour. The old order amounted to exactly the same thing if properly enforced, so that it is obvious that the Committee cannot see any necessity for making 9, change in the conditions governing the work let to contractors. Councillor Stott is just as much dissatisfied with the sub-committee's recom- mendations as with the old standing order, and in a series of special motions .he frames an order embodying the Trade Unto: rate of wages. All parties concerned are, there- fore, exactly where they were at the commence- ment, and it is high time, we think, that the Council settled the question absolutely. The Trade Unionists have i had more than fair consider- ation shown to them n the matter,and it is appar- ently impossible to convince them that desirable as it may seem to them to have a Trade Uniou Clause it would not operate as fairly and equably' as the one now in force. The printing trade locally affords an excellent example. If Mr. Stott's motion was carried about 90 per cent. of the printers 111 Warrington would decline to touch Corporation work, and yet no one could assert that the conditions of labour as imposed by them are not all that can be reasonably expected. The Council will be wasting further time by discussing Councillor Stott's motions. The principle involved is perfectly well understood and need not ha further elaborated.

We were somewhat in error last week in stating that the Council had shelved the question of asking' Mr. Carnegie for a grant in aid of the museum and library. The matter was evidently put on one side for the moment to obviate what promised CO he a somewhat acrimonious discussion at the last Council meeting. Nevertheless we hardly thought the Museum Committee would have had the temerity to re-introduce the subject just yet. The Committee is evidently ince an importunate mendicant and not easily deterred Moreover the General Purposes Committee has passed a resolution submitted by the Museum Committee authorising the opening of communi.- cations with Mr. Carnegie, so that the question as to whether Warrington shall beg from the Pitts- ' burg ironrnaster is half-decided. A month ago Ithe Mayor was strongly opposed to ani such appeal being made, and Councillor T. Wright was even more averse to any of Mr. Carnegie's money coming to Warrington by such a channel. There will probably be an illuminat- ing discossion on the subject next Tuesday, with not a little historical quotation which may ps pungent. After the paragraph which went the round of the papers last m3nth we should imagine that Mr. Carnegie will firmly and perhaps not politely refuse any request from Warrington. But we hope the Council will not give him the oppor- tunity. To say the least at this stage, it is beneath the dignity of a County Borough to solicit alms from an American millionaire for a public improve' ment.

Accident at Vulcan Foundry. - Joe Bailey, 29 years of age, residing at Manchester- road, Vulcan, met with a serious accident at Vulcan Foundry on Monday. He was at work on some machinery when his left arm was caught and wrenched off while he also sustained an injury to his head. He was at once removed to the 'Warrington Infirmary, where he was detained.

Warrington Corporation Official's Success. Warrington will shortly lose the services of Mr.J.W. Howarth, who for many years has beld the office of sub-librarian, and who at the end of July will take over the duties of librarian and curator at Chelmsford. The Chelmsford library is the outcome ot a gift from Mr. Andrew Carnegie and is at present in course of construction. There were almost 100 applicants for the appointment which has been obtained by Mr. Howarth, the commencing salary for which is £l5O a year. Mr. Howarth has been employed at the War- rington library for 14 years.

The Football Club " Smoker." Preparations for the Football Club smoking concert in celebration of the past season's victories are being rapidly pushed forward. The members have taken to the idea, and the event is likely to be an important one. A meeting of the sub-committee was held on Tuesday, and it was decided to engage Parr Hall, retain the body of the ball for the 800 members, and charge for the use of the galleries. ,An excellent musical programme will be arranged, and during the evening the players of both Ist and "A" teams will receive their medals. Mr. W. R. Neil (the president) is expected to preside. Refreshments will be provided. A Warrington Agent's Thefts. At the St. Helens Police Court on Monday, James Dl`Collum, of Dickenson-street, Warrington, pleaded guilty to a charge of stealing a pair of slippers from the shop of a pawnbroker upon whom he had called to solicit an order for a sewing machine, and to stealing a quantity of braid, handkerchiefs, and tablecloths from the shop of Miss Halton, Borough-road.—The Chief Constable said the man's wife was very respect- able, and she had informed him that they had pre- viously been in a good position, but owing to drink the prisoner had brought ruin upon them.—The magis- trates fined the prisoner 21s. or a month on each charge.

A Warrington Journalist in America. The " Newspaper Owner" in a current issue states that : " Some years ago Mr. Fredßirchall, son of Mr. T. Birchall, J.P., of Warrington, entered the journal- istic profession, commencing on "The Warrington Examiner," a well-known paper in that town, and, after gaining valuable experience, he obtained a situation as reporter on a leading journal in Cambridge. In the university city he further perfected himself in his profession, and then left England to try his fortunes in America, and after some trying experiences he gained a firm position and became a member of the staff of "The New York Sun," and rose to the position of city editor, Owing to the illness of a mem- ber of his family at home, he lately came home on a I visit, and he has now gone back to join another of the Idailies of New York, as night editor, at a salary equivalent to £7OO a year. Mr F. Birchall was a I student of the Warrington Grammar School, an institu- tion that has sent out men to fulfil important positions in various parts of the world." Ascension Day Observance. The observance of Ascension Day on Thursday was on much the same lines as in previous years. The managers of six Church of England schools—Fairfield, Warrington Parochial, Hamilton-street, Ladies' School of Industry, St. James's Latchford, and St. Peter's— had applied to the School Attendance Committee for permission to close the schools for the whole day. This conression was refused, but the managers were informed that they could keep the schools closed until 9.so—the time ordinarily set apart for religious instruction. All the school managers who had applied, with the exception of St. Peter's, availed themselves of this opportunity of taking the scholars to church, and the ordinary school lessons commenced at 9.50. By per- mission of the, committee. the whole of the Roman Catholic Schools were closed for the day, as Ascension' Day is recognized as being "exclusively set apart for religious observance" by members of that communion. Prospectus. In another column we publish the notice of prospec- tus of Peter Walker and Son Property Corporation N 0.2 Limited, which has been formed for the purpose of taking over various licensed and other properties which have been acquired during recent years, and in two cases have just been " agreed to be acquired by Peter Walker and Son (Warrington and Burton) Limited. The corporation are offering for sub- scription at par £170,000 guaranteed first mortgage registered debenture stock, and part of an issue of £250,000, bearing interest at the rate of £4 per cent. per annum. This issue is secured not only by the trust deed of first mortgage and debentures upon the free- hold, copyhold and leasehold licensed and other pro- perties, but also by the guarantee for principal and interest of Peter Walker and Son, (Warrington and Burton), Limited. The guaranteeing company were converted into a limited company in 1890, with a capital of £1,000,000, in debentures bearing interest at 4 per cent, per annum, £1,000,000 in preference shares entitled to a cumulative preferential dividend of £5 per cent. per annum, and £1,000,000 in ordinary shares. The ordinary shares and one third of the debentures and preference shares were on such conversion retained by the late Sir Andrew B. Walker, Bart., and his 'nominees. The certificate of the auditors (Messrs. Harmood Banner and Son) states that since the formation of the company £1,200,000 has been applied out of profits, to reserve and guarantee funds and in reduction of 'goodwill. The list will open on Tuesday, June 6th, and close at any time aereafter on or before Thursday, June Bth at 3 o'clock.

Free Use of the Baths for School Children.

_ School children will no doubt be delighted to hear that in future they are to be allowed the free use of the Corporation baths once each week. At a meeting of tbe Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee, a depu- tation from the School Management Committee of the Education Committee attended to make some arrange- ment on the matter. After some discussion it was decided that on payment of g,50 per aanum by the Edu- cation Committee the school children of the borough shall be allowed to use the baths free of charge three afternoons a week duriny the season. A rota will be arranged, and it is expected that about 1,000 children will avail themselves of the privilege each week.

GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. , Exciting Rescue Scene at Howley. _ .

On Saturday a man named Parry had a very narrow escape from drowning in the river at Howley. He and a man named Taylor, bath of whom are employed at Messrs. Rylands Bros., made a match to swim a certain distance in the river for a small wager. Both stripped and entered: the water. Parry got Out of his_depth, and was in danger of being drowned, when a young man named Boyle, hearing cries of distress, ran out of an adjoining tannery where he was employed, and jumped in to the rescue. He also got into difficulties, and a man named Phillips residing in Hopwood-street, went to his assistance, and succeeded in bringing Boyle to the bank, but Parry had drifted some distance away. Philips swam after him, and succeeded in seizing him by t he hair. After considerable difficulty he brought him to the bank, and both men were taken home. But for the appearance of Philips both men would have been &owned.

H, M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC dispels low spirits.— Advt. Warrington Polyglot Society. The members of this Society had their first excur- sion on Saturday last, when a considerable number journeyed by cycle and 'bus to Norton Priory and Halton Castle. Permission bad been granted by Sir Richard Brooke to see the various objects of interest, and the privilege was much appreciated. The visitors were met by the head gardener, Mr. Slater, who con- ducted them through the gardens and grounds. The rhododendrons were not yet at their best, the recent east winds having retarded the blooms, but enough was seen to ensure a fine display shortly. Inspection was .nade of the old Crypt, and at this point Mr. May, president of the society, was able to give some inter- esting details as to the various odjects to be seen. After saying goodbye to the Priory, the party pro- ceeded to Halton Castle,and after justice had been done to the inner man or woman, the services of the Society's photographer were called into requisition, with, it is to be hoped satisfactory results. Home was reached in good time, all 'cherishing pleasant recollections of a satisfactory day out. "PREMIER" CYCLES at 9 gtine,as for Best Grade, unsurpassed.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. Dr. Agar Beet's Resignation. The Rev. Dr. Agar Beet, the theological tutor at Richmond Wesleyan college, whose contributions to the study of eschatology have attracted much attention of late years; has at last resigned his post theie. The announcement to many in his own church and outside it says the" Manchester Guardian" wilt come as a relief, foi• since the " heresy hunt" which followed the publication of his book on " The Last Things " it had been felt that tie compromise then arrived at was not satisfactory. The position was that while Dr. Beet had not changed his views he had partly pledged himself not to teach them, though of course they were perfectly well known to his students and to the A professor holding his office on condition that he must not declare his whole mind must often have uncomfortable moments. Dr. Beet has now decided that he cannot any lenger consent to the suppression of his book on" The Last Things," and so he has resigned. A new edition of the book will shortly be published.--Dr. Beet worked in the Warrington Wesleyan circuit about twenty‘years ago, and quite recently preached special sermons at Tewsey Road Wesleyan Church, at the same time answering ques- tiods on difficulties of belief. TARE H.M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC and be Influenza Proof .—Advt. Prize Distribution at Padgate. The prizes gained by the students at the National school evening classes, Padgate, were distributed at a social gathering last Friday evening. There was a large attendance of pupils and friends assembled, and a pleasant time was spent in dancing, Sze., several songs and recitations forming a pleasing diversion. The songs " The chorister" and "The longshoreman" were excellently rendered by Arthur Griffiths, one of the pupils, and Miss Houghton was very loudly applauded for a couple of recitations. Mr. S. Critchley sang " I was born on a Friday "in an approptiately doleful .style. Mr. Blackwell, the bead teacher, congratulated the pupils on the splendid attendance which had been made during ie past session —no fewer than twenty-four having qualified for prizes by having made at least a minimum of 90 per cent, of the possible hours, while 12 had actually made 100 per cent. Mr. G. Mather, in presenting the prizes, spoke encouraging words to the recipients and urged them to continue their studies. Later, in responding to a vote of thanks, which was proposed by the Vicar, Mr. Mather said it was possible that in the near future a change would be made in the administration of the svening schools in the Lancashire County area. The winners of full attendance prizes were Emma Malpas, Enid Mellor, Eunice Mellor, Dora Mellor, Elsie Horridge, Brenda Peters, Sylvanus Pass, William Heath, George Rigbey, Georgv Pass, Harold Tomlin, and William Pass, while Dorothy London, Alice Heath and Osborne Moss were absent for one evening only.
