UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BOOM—Advt. Child's Fatal Fall. The circumstances attending the -death of Robert Charles Looker, aged three years, son of Robert Looker, wire worker, 33, Longshaw street, were inquired into before Coroner Brighouse on Monday. According to the evidence, deceased was in the street a noon on Thursday in company with his sister, aged sox. The girl was in front of him and in trying to catch up to her deceased stumbled and fell, his head coming into contact with the tiles of the footpath. His mother bathed his head and he appeared to be going on satisfactorily until Satarday morning when he complained of severe pains in the head. Dr. Geary was sent for and attended hhn until he died on Sunday morning. —A verdict of "Accidental death "was returned.

Compositor Found Drowned. Early' on Tuesday morning the body of Edwin Home (20), compositor, 4, Percy-street, was recovered from the St. Helens and Sankey Canal at Sankey.— The inquest was held at the Chapel House Inn, Great Sankey, on Wednesday evening by Mr. S. Brighouse. County Coroner. James Horne, father of the deceased said his • son had been missing from home since May 22nd. Recently he had been working at Cadishead and witness was under the impression that he was still employed there until Monday last. It was subse- quently ascertained by P.S. Banister that he left there on May 6th. Deceased had wandered about a good deal and was a little eccentric. Some Nw months ago he was much given to studying religious subjects—preparing papers, &c. Witness had haver had the slightest reason to suspect that he would take his life.—Alfred Mather, plumber, Mill-Tane, spoke to recovering the body from the canal.--P.C. Houghton said deceased appeared to have been in the water four days or a week. A piece of paper bearing a number of texts was found in his ,pocket.—The Coroner remarked that from what the cilice had been informed, deceased had done queer things lately and he appeared to have had a touch of religions mania.— A verdict of " Found drowned" was returned.

HEALTH OF THE BOROUGH. Dr. Gornall's Annual Report. THE PAVING OF STREETS AND PASSAGES.

Dr. J. Guest Gornall, ALA ALB., has just presented his report on the state of the public health for 1904. We make the following interesting extracts :—Some important changes have been brought about during the year in this Department, including the adoption of the recommendations made last year as to the staff. Of those recommendations, the provision of better office accommodation is still in abeyance, but the two other things, the appointment of a public analyst and of two additional inspectors, are accomplished facts. This is

A GREAT STEP IN -ADVANCE. Mr. Rnddock, the public analyst appointed took up his I duties in April last, and furnishes a report on his work during the remainder of the year. Not only has he found the large proportion of 1703 per cent among the articles examined to have been more or less adulterated, but he has brought to light how widely the habit of I using chemical preservatives is spreading. Last year the widespread nuisance of UNPAVED MEETS AND PASSAGES I was specially reported on. lam glad that such great efforts have been made during the year to cope with this difficulty and so many streets and passages paved, not chiefly owing, I believe to what I said, but in deference to wide dissatisfaction with a state of things which was notorious. Bat among the nuisances remain- ing unabated it is my duty to -call the attention of the Sanitary Authority to the want of paving in those streets and passages in the list of last year that have not yet received attention (some of them of very old standing) as well as in some omitted from that list, and in others that have since come into existence. What is really wanted, as I pointed out last year; in some more definite system, under which it should be impossible for streets and passages to remain for years in such an unsatisfactory state as they do. In conclusion, says Dr. Gornall, though the state of Warrington is as yet by no means such that my annufil report can form an advertisement of its healthiness, I think thNl3 will be found in the pages that follow, abundant evidence that the Health Committee are endeavouring in face of great difficulties, made no easier by the new demands for education expenditure, etc., to bring about very considerable improvements in the place. DECLINE OF THE BIRTH BATE.

Going into details Dr, Gomall- -Vtt-tie-s :—The Birth- rate for 1901 is the lowest yet recorded, having fallen from 356 per 1,000 in 1003, to 32'7, and comparing very unfavourably with the average of the previous ten years, which is 37•1 per thousand, living. The causes of this decline, which is common to the whole country, and, indeed to most European nations, are probably of various kinds, among which must be reckoned, besides the deferring of the age of marriage and the use of measures to prevent cono4tion, a more mysterious influence—a declinin7, reproductivity in the race. THE DEATH RATE.

HEALTH CONDITION OF.THE TOWN COMPARES UNFAVOURABLY WITH THE COUNTY AS A

WHOLE.

Dr. Gornall stites thai-iie—n--e.t number of deaths at all ages belonging to the district was 1,334. The net death rate was therefore equal to lil.4—a figure which, though of value for comparing the healthiness of the town in 1904 with that in previous years, is misleading when used relatively to other places. The reason of this says Dr. Gornall is that the age distribution of the population that has so important a bearing on mortality Is not taken into account. With a view to obviating this want of exactness, so that the death-rate of a manufacturing town litte Warrington can be fairly compared with that of a health resort, both being re- duced to flue standard, viz., the age distributon in Eng- land and Wales at the census of 1901, the Registrar General has introduced for each place a factor for correction. The net death-rate for Warrington in 1904, viz., 19.4, multiplied by 1.0791 (the factor for Warring- ton) gives n.; the corrected death-rate for the year as 21.023, or roughly 21—an unsatisfactorily high figure. ZYMOTIC AND INFANTILE DEATH RATE.

The unusual height of the z-v-m—otic —dealt; rate (4•6) was mainly due to 52 deaths from scarlet fever, SG from whooping cough, and MI from diarrhcea. 835 deaths occurred under one year of ige. Undoubt- edly many of the cases were preventable deaths due to ignorance (more often, Dr. Gornall fears, culpable than not) on the part of the mothers. When the department is able to obtain fuller information of the distribution of births in the town, it will be polsible to visit a 'greater number, and at an earlier period than has been done. Dr. Gornall goes on to deal with infectious diseases advocating the provision of a public cleansing and dis- infecting station, and the completion of the smallpox hospital, and concludes by suggesting measures neces- sary to further improve the sanitary condition of the day schools, and enchance their usefulness for educa- tional purposes.

BISCUITS

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

Business of the Next Meeting.

The monthly meeting of the Town Council will be held on Tuesday evening, when the following among other business will be considered:

OVERDUE STREET-MARINO 'ACCOUNTS.

The Borough Treasurer submitted a list of overdue accounts for street-making. Resolved, That the Town Clerk take proceedings for the recovery of the same,and that in such cases as he may deemat advisable, Mr. Alfred Clive be appointed Receiver on behalf of the Corporation. -

OPEN-AIR SWII:4MING BATHS AND FREE BATHING. Councillor Stott will ask the Chairman of the Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee :-1. Has the Com- mittee yet considered the question of providing open- air swimming baths, in accordanoe with the resolution unanimously passed by the Council at a meeting held on October 25th, 1904? 2. If the, question has been considered, what decision has the committee arrived at? 3. If the question hss not yet been considered, will he undertake that the question shall receive the consideration of the committee at an early date, and report their decision to the Council. The committee has resolved that this committee allow school children to use the Ist class plunge bath on Tuesdays, Wednesdays, and Fridays, from 3.30 to 6 p.m., on payment by the Education Committee of £5O per annum, subject to a teacher being in charge of the children.—The deputation from the Education Com- mittee intimated their willingness to accept these terms. WATER COMMITTEE:I-COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON AND LA.wSON'S CONTRACT.

Councillor Pemberton will ask the Chairman of the Water Committee :

1. Has Lawson's %ill for all work and extras been received? If so. what date and what is the amount ? 2. Has it been before the Water Committee? If not, why not? 3. Have any legal proceedings been threatened?

_ Councillor Pemberton will also move that Mr. Dens, the Water Engineer, shall shors.Councillor Pemberton all the papers, contracts, and letters in connection with Lawson's contract at Delph-lane, iny other member of the Council being present as a witness ; that a com- mittee be appointed consisting of J. Pemberton. Alder- man Pierpoint, Councillors Lord, Forshaw, J. Roberts, and Barton. to inquire into the very large amount of extras on the Delph-lane contraot, the reason of same and report findings to the Town Council.

PHYSICAL INSTRUCTION FOR ELEMENTARY SCHOLARS. The Gymnasium Committee on May 30, carefully con- sidered the report of the School Management Com- mittee with regard to the proposed physical instruction to be given to children attending Public Eleinentar,y Schools, and they regret that the School Management Committee have not adhered to the terms agreed upon in the original report submitted to the Council. This committee are of opinion that the proposals contained in the original report if they had been adhered to would have conduced to more eflicient instruction and ensured uniformity of instruction among the classes. Resolved that a shield be provided for the Champion Eight for 1905.

ELECTRICITY AND TRA.MWAYS-DICREASE OF SALARIES..

This committee has resolved that the salary of Mr. J. Telfer, tramways manager, be increased from £l3O to £l5O per annum; that the salary of Mr. F. W. Purse, assistant electrical engineer, be increased from £1513 to £l7O per annum ; that the salary of Mr. W. E. Rogers, mains engineer, be increased from 2133 to £l4O per lannum.

Resolved—that the Town Clerk apply to the Board of Trade fdr an order prolonging the time for the com- pletion of the tramways. Resolved—that the tram lines in Manchester-road be extended to the far side of Padgate-lane, and that the Borough Surveyor be requested to undertake the work.

THE APPLICATION TO M. CARNEGIE. _

The following resolution of the Museum Committee was considered and approved by the General Purposes ComMittee : That the Library Sub-Committee be authorised to open communication with Mr. Andrew Carnegie with a view to ascertain whether he would consider an application for a grant in order to extend the Library, either by an enlargement of the preient Museum and Library or by another building."

THE FAIR WAGES' CLAUSE.

Alderman Tinnion and Councillors, Evans, Maginn, P. Peacock, Pemberton, Stott and Wilkinson, the Sub- Committee appointed to consldet•the. Standing Orders relating to rates of wages and hour of labour in Cor- poration Contracts report that they have, held three meetings. They took into •.consideration Standing Order N 0.43 dealing with the rates of wages and hours iof labour observed n Corporation Contracts and the Town Clerk submitted returns of information which he had obtained from other. Towns, from which it appeared that in 44 towns there was no such Standing Order in force; 14 towns, Trades Union wages, &c.' were observed; 25 towns, wages, &C., as agreed between representatives of masters and men ; 56 towns, a standard fair rate of wages in force. Your Committee are of opinion that the Standard rate of wages and recognised hours of labour should be observed in all Corporation Contmctsand sub-Contracts and beg to recommend the adoption of the following Standing Order in substitution for the present one That in all contracts and sub-contracts the contractor shall pay all persons engaged or employed by them on Corporation work the standard. rate of wages recognised in the district, and shall observe such hours of labour as are generally accepted as fair in each traie, and shall 'from time to time whenever, required so to do by the council.prodnce to them such evidence as they may deem sufficient that they are paying such wages and observing such hours. No contract with the corpora- tion shall be sub-let to any parson. who does not comply with this standing order. Every breach of this standing order shall be taken into consideration Iby the councit before entering into any further con- tract with the person who committed the breach. Councillor Stott will move :-1. That in all contracts and sub-contracts the contractors shall pay all persons engaged or employed by them the Trades Union rate of wages recognised in the district where the work is being carried out, etc. Should there be no Trades Union rate of wages or hours of labour in the district where the work is being executed, the Trades Union rate and conditions of labour in the nearest district to where the work is being done shall be observed by the contractors. Contractors executing works for this Council shall not prohibit their workpeople from join- ing trades societies or from continuing members of such societies.

CORPORATION UNDERTAKINGS.

Reports on the Work of the ° Electricity and Water Departments.

Mr. F. V. Mathias, Borough Electrical and Tramways Engineer, has presented a report on the working of the I electric supply undertaking during the year ending March 31st. It is with considerable satisfaction he states, that he calls the attention of the Committee to the improvement in the working of the electric supply undertaking, as disclosed by the revenue account for the year, notwithstanding the general depression of trade. In connection with the revenue account, it should also be taken into consideration that a rednction in charges for current to the extent of £624, 19s. Ild, has been made to the tramways as compared with lust year, also a decrease equivalent to 2093 per cent, to public light- ing, and the contribution to interest and sinking fund charges has been inEreased by £540 9s. 10d. in excess of last year. The gross revenue amounts to .212,580 195 8d; working expenses, £6,075 14s. Gd. ; leaving a balance of £6,505 ss. 2d. Out of this has been paid :—lnterest on capital, £2,461 us. Ild. ; sinking fund, £2,275 19s. 9d. ; rates, income tax &c.,.£224 Os. Bd.; leaving net profit on year's working, £1,543 l. 9d. • as against £l2B 14s. 3d. for the previous year. During the year the equivalent of 8,345 c.-p. lamps have been added to the mains. The in- crease is very satisfactory, and shows that electriCity,as an illuminating agent and a source of power is popular, and being freely adopted. Th- total revenue from private lighting and power is £12,070 Is., representing 1,482,456 units, 288,261 units for lighting, and 1,194,195 for power, being an increase of £l,OBl Sc. 3d. and 283,455 units over last year, the average yield being 1'954 pence per unit, which is a low figure, and compares very favourably with other towns. The cost of production show sa material reduction per unit as compared with last year. In the Tramways Department,the Borough Treasurer reports a profit of £45085. 6d. which reduces last year's deficit from ,C 692 is. 7d. to £241 192.1 d.

THE WATER. DEPARTMENT.

In a report on the waterworks department Mr. James Deas, water engineer,states that he estimates the popu- lation supplied with water at about 90,000. The exten- sion of mains to meet further requirements has steadily continued, the greater part of the extensions being in districts outside the Borough. The past year has seen the completion of the works authorised under the Bill of 1899. Work was commenced in the autumn of 1899, and may be divided into three sections : Houghton Green, Winwick, and Appleton. The villages of Burtonwood,, Collins Green Houghton Green, Hermitage Green, Croft, Culcheth, Kenyon, Newchurch, Glazebury, Grappenhall, Thelwall. Stretton and Daresbury, representing a population (1901 Census) of 8,000, have been coupled up to the mains,' and these ' extensions of mains should prove, in the near future, prat bearing expenditure, 'as nearly all the villages, especially on the Lancashire side, are growing at a fair rate. Notwithstanding the arduous character of the new works there has been no loss of life and no.I serious accident during the five yearS the works were in I progress. Now that the station is in full running con- dition, the level of the water in the wells is very satis- factory ; after 12 hours' stop the rest level is 44 feet below ground level, and after 12 hours' pumping at the rate\of 3 millions per 24 hours, the water is lowered 50 feet in the well.

MR. DAVID WILCOX,.

I We record with regret the death of Mr. David. Wilcox, an old Warringtonian which occurred on Monday at his , residence, 125, Wilderspool Causeway. Mr.Wilcox,who was in his 58th year, had been ailing for some consider- ! able time and about five weeks ago his complaint,, of an internal character, took an acute turn, and he died as above stated on Monday. Early in life he entered the service of Messrs.Thewlis and Griffiths and earned the confidence of his employers, being, some 32 years ago, appointed manager of the file department. The death occurred at his residence, Wilderspool Causeway, on Monday, of Mr. William Dick, who fort quarter of a century held the office of correspondidg secretary to Messrs. Pearson and Knowles. Mr. Dick, who is in his 67th year, was the son of a Congregational minister and was a native of Eaglesham, near Glasgow. He came to Warrington in 1871 and subsequently entered the service of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles. He retired about nine years ago and entered the service of the Refuge Friendly Society, attending to his duties until a week prior to his death. He was a member of th 3. Wycliffe Congregational Church and was a regular attendant at that place of worship. He leaves four children and his death will be regretted by a large circle of friends.

JAMBS TOOL& Many Warrington people will regret to hear of the , death of Mr. James Toole, of Allen-street, who passed ' away somewhat suddenly on Monday. The deceased gentleman who was 63 years of age, appeared to be in good health up to last week. On Sunday he was seized with aniapoplectic fit which proved fatal. He was attended by Dr. Fox and Dr. Stewart, of Liverppol. Mr. Toole was a native of Guilford, County Armagh, Ireland, and came to Warrington in 1870. He commenced business in a small way as a marine store dealer in Rylands-street. The concern gradually developed until it reached its present high position, being well established all over the country. Mr. Toole was a staunch Roman Catholic and for a considerable period had been a worshipper at St. Alban's Church. He was a member of the committee of the Catholic Young Men's Society. In politics he was a Liberal and a strong supporter of the Irish cause. He was a prominent member of the Liberal Club and took considerable interest in the affairs of that organization. He leaves a widow and eight children with whom much sympathy is felt in their bereaveme.-.t. I _ _ _ THE FtiN::RAL.

- By the death,. which occurred on Sunday under pain- fully sudden circumstances, of Mr. Joseph Mountleld, ' of Ivy Lea, Walton Inferior, there has been removed a gentleman who was widely 'ir.nown and highly respected in the district. For some One the deceased gentleman, ' who was 54 years of age, had been in a delicate state of 1 health, and decided to pay a visit to the Canary Islands. I He was in a weak condition, and was advised by some of his friends not to venture so far from home' but he was under the impression that the visit might be beneficial. He MIS away about four weeks, but on reaching England on Sunday, before the boat landed at England,be became seriously ill and died very suddenly. He was seen standing, travelling bag in hand, ready to step on shore. Later he was missed, and was subsequently found dead in a chair. It is supposed that the excitement connected with the home coming was too much for him and that death was due to heart disease. In early life Mr. Mounfield carried on business as a builder. but latterly he retired from active association with the business, handing the management over to , his son. Mr. Alounfield took a deep interest in all ; public work and at the time of his death held several ' important offices. He was a member of the Runcorn I Board of Guardians and the Runcorn Rural District ' Council, being at one time elected to preside over the deliberations of the last named authority. On both bodies he was highly esteemed by his colleagues, his straightforward business like methods commanding the respect of all. He was also a member of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee and of the Walton Parish Council displaying great interest in the work of both bodies Mr. Mountield was an enthusiastic Liberal and rendered 1. valuable help in the work in the Rnutsford division. In religion he was an Independent Methodist and was I closely identified with the Methodist Church at Stockton Heath, playing no small part in the scheme which culminated in the erection of the new place of worship. He leaves a widow, one son and two I daughters.

REFERENCE AT THE RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

At the fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of' Guardians, at Dutton, on Tuesday, the Chairman (Alder- man A. R. Norman) said it was his melancholy duty to refer to the unfortunate death of one of their colleagues Mr. Mounfield. The news cameto them more suddenly, , remembering the fact that at the last meeting they had word that Mr. Monnfield was going on very well, and was better for the sea trip he had had. Unfortun. ately, the sad news was that he died either while arriving or just on arrival in this country. It was always a sad thing to have a visitation of this kind, and especially in one who, although struggling with physical ailments, always tried to do his public duty. ' That was one of the best features about Mr, Moninield. He was a thoroughly con. scientious man, and he tried to do his public duty to the very last. He (the chairman) was sorry to say %hat

gtome in robust health and strength did not give their services as they might do to public work, and, as ,he had said, it was rather pathetic in this I case that Mr.Mounfield,although having a long struggle to retain his health, never let go his public duty. John Wesley MIS once asked what Ile would do if he knew that the angel of death would visit him on the follow- ing day. lie said be should take out his diary, see what engagements he had, and carry them out just as if nothing was going to happen. It seemed to him that Mr. Mounfield had to some extent in that position. and always had their sympathy. He had always tried to carry out his duty. This must be a solace and com- fort to the relatives and friends he had left behind. He was sure he was only expressing the members' wishes when he offered their deepest sympathy to the family in their sad bereavement.

A resolution to this effect was passed in silence, the members standing. MRS. EDWARD BOLTON. The deceased lady. who was well known and deeply respected, was in her 81st year.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Wright —Atkinson.

There was a large congregation at the Parish Church on Thurday on the occasion of the marriage of Miss Elizabeth Ellen Wright, younger daughter of the late Alderman Wright, J.P., a former Mayor of the Borough, and Mr. James Higinbottom Atkinson, Newenden,Kent, son of the late Mr. J. IL Atkinson, Carlisle. The bride who was given away by her brother, Mr. J. H. Wright, Middlesborough, was gowned ilia simple dress of ivory crepe-de-chine, and wore a veil of old Brussels lace over a wreath of orange blossom. She carried a bouquet of choice white flowers, She was attended by her sister, Miss Wright, her niece, Miss Lilian Swallow, and Miss Irene Kent-Smith, cousin of the bridegroom. Miss Wright was attired in a dress of champagne coloured Limerick lace over silk, with hat to match, and carried a bouquet of sweet peas. The two children were in white with silk caps and carried dainty baskets of flowers. Miss Wright wore a gold curb bracelet, and the two other bridesmaids wore lockets, the gift of the bridegroom. The ceremony was conducted by the Revs. A. F. White and T. W. Barber, Mr. F. M. Darby was at the organ and played several appropriate selections, Mr. E. E. Selmes, Newenden was the best man. The reception was held at the house of the bride's mother, 42, Wilson-Patten-street, and later in Vie day the happy couple left for the North where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was of dark blue cloth with white facings and she wore a hat to match.

PRESENTATION FROM THE ELECTRICITY DEPARTMENT.

On Saturday morning Mr. Mathias was presented by the Electricity and Tramways Department with a wedding present of a silver tea service. The presentation was made at the Electric Lighting Station. Mr. F. IV. Purse, the chief assistant engineer, said they had met to present their respected chief with a silver tea service to show their good will and respect towards him. (Applause.) He was very pleased to have been associated with him, and thought the work at the Electric Lighting Station had been carried on very successfully, and could compare favourably with any other station in the North of England. He was sure they all wished Mr. Mathias much happiness and success. (Applause.) Mr. J. Telfer (tramways manager) said he had known Mr. Mathias for about twelve months, and he hoped he would be as successful in his new undertaking as he had been in his Corporation work. (Laughter and applause.). In making the presentation, Mr. Purse said that, in , the name of the Electricity and Tramways Department, he wished to hand over the present. There was vet to come a tray to match the present, which would be handed over to Mr. Mathias in a few days. He hoped Mr. Mathias would be happy, successful and prosperous, and never regret the time he had spent in Warrington. (Applause.) Mr. Mathias, returned thanks, and said he valued the present very much, not only for its beauty, but especi- ally for the good feeling and kind wishes they had expressed towards him. As they no doubt knew 'about that time of the year he had to render an account of the department committed to his charge, and he was able to render a very favourable one. He thought that was entirely due to the loyalty of his staff. (Applause.) As Mr. Purse had remarked, they had forced their way up as one of the best stations in the North of England,and he hoped they would not rest on their laurels, but still go on quite as well as in ti e past, They should recognize that they were helping the traders and manufacturers ins very keen and tierce com- petition, and there was no doubt that any good work put in was bound to tell, not only for the town, but for the country. Judging from the way in which they had sup- ported him last year they would this year be able to help the town very materially. (Applause). Three cheers were then given for Mr. Mathias.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT BAPTIST ASSOCIATION. Meetings at Galborne.

On Wednesday afternoon and evening the Spring meetings in connection with the above association were held at Golbornea In the afternoon a Conference was held at which the ministers and delegates from the Warrington, Lymm, Hill Cliff e, Latchford, Little Legh and Golbome Baptist Churches were present. A very satisfactory report of the work proceeding at the new Mission Church at Barnton was presented and the asso- ciation took into consideration the advisability of extending their woliiin this respect in other fields of labour. It was alsolfecided to take steps to form in connection with the association a Lay Preachers Union and a committee to take the preliminary steps was appointed. It was also arranged'to hold the next meetings of the association at Acton Bridge and the Rev. S. NV. Back and Mr. Beswick was appointed to speak.

After the conference a very interesting paper was reas by the Rev. A. J. W. Back of Lyrum and was much appre- ciated by his audlence.—After.a short discursion on the paper, tea was then served in the schoolroom the table oeing presided over by Mrs. Beswiek and Mrs. Reeves. of Golborne. At 7 o'clock a Public Meeting was held at which the Rev. J. S. Hughes, the President of the Association took the chair. Mr. Hughes in the course of his open- ing address explained the work and objects of the association as this was the first series of meetings held at Golborne under the auspices of the Association. The Rev. Ivor 11. James, of Atherton, then addressed the meeting, and in the course of a vigorous and able speech, propounded the principles of Nonconformity, in, general, and by the Baptists in particular, and stated that it was not so much that they held to principles, but that the principle held them and impelled them to work and struggle for religious equality, and the mOral social improvement of their fellow man. Mr. England, of Warrington, gave an interesting historical address on the workaccomplished by Baptists, citing among others John Bunyan (author of Pi:grim's Progress), William Carey, the first missionary to India, the work of the American Baptists in taking the lead for the abolition of slavery, and stated that the Baptists were ever in the van struggling on behalf of the people for civil and religions liberty, exemplified to-day in the persons of Dr. Clifford and Mr. tloyd-George.—Mr. England also said that the Baptists were always ready to go hand in hand with all other religious bodies in the work of social reform and for the uplifting and. improvment of the conditions of the people. , At the close of the addresses thanks were rendered to the speakers and a series of successful and enjoyable ' meetings brought to a close by the singing of the Doxology.

THEFTS BY A SERVANT. At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, Mary Mi'linger, a servant in the employ of Mr. John Magian, fis, Bridge street, was charged with stealing from the dwelling-house and storeroom between May Ist and 27th Mur pairs of boots, 01). of tea, a quantity of soap, several pieces of lace, trimmings, &c., in all of the value of 20s. Superintendent Monaghan said the pris- oner entered Mr. Maginn'e employ on May Ist as a domestic servant and remained there until Saturday. Mrs. Maginn became suspicions of her and searched her bedroom and found several of the above articles con- cealed under the mattress. She had also been seen to send a parcel containing a pair of boots to her married sister at Runcorn on May 17th. She said she had bought them in the market. On a later day she sent another pair of boots to a man at Western Point. When charged with the thefts she admitted them. Mr. Maginn said her parents were respectable people and he did not wish to press the case. Prisoner was fined 205.. or in default of payment, fourteen days imprisonment.

ball so near the object ball, it is almost as easy to get the cannon by means of a thin screw as by taking the object ball pretty full. How infinitely superior the thin screw is to the fullish contact screw is shown by the after position, for whereas the fullish contact drives :he object hall on to the top cushioa and then lowa the table, the thin screw drives it slant- ingly across the table, and a well-played stroke will leave the three balls grouped together within a short distance of the corner pocket, ind the position will generally be a splendid :me. On the diagram the thin intersected line ;bows the good direction given to the object ball as the result of the thin contact, and the thick intersected line the bad direction which results from the fullish contact. Early on in this chapter on SCREW I stated that the correct way of playing screw-back strokes is to drive the cue clean through the ball instead of drawing it hack sharply, as so many players always do. An exception must be made to this rule, however. when the cue ball is very close to the object ball.

DIAGRAM l'A.

Diagram 108 shows such a screw-back cannon, Here the cue ball is only one inch from the red, and were the cue driven through the cue ball, the -stroke would result in a push. Even without driving the cue through the ball, this close screw-back cannon is often a failure with many really good amateurv. When the stroke fails, the cue ball generally jumps, and most players conclude that the jumping has been caused by a miss-cue. As a rule, however, there has been no miss-cue, and the jumping of the cue ball has been caused by its recoiling from the object balls on to the cue. The cue has either followed on a little or has not been got out of the way quickly enough, and the cue ball has had such a little distance to recoil before meeting the cue again that the cue striking the ball, and the ball striking the cue, appear to be one and the same stroke. Especially is this the case when the cue follows on the least little bit, for in this case it meets the cue ball practically as it comes away from the object ball. The manner in which the cue ball jumps as it meets the cue may be made quite clear by playing a ball across the table and meeting it with the point of the cue (held low down) as it returns from the opposite cushion. . .

When playing close screw-backs similar to the one shown on Diagram 108, care must therefore be taken to draw the cue back immediately it strikes the ball. The stroke should not be played with any strength; all tnat is necessary is a smart little stroke. Nor need the cue ball be struck very low down. It naturally follows that the more gently the stroke can be played, the easier will it be to draw back the cue at the moment of contact. Owing to the balls being so utar, practically none of the reverse rotation imparted to the cue ball is lost by friction with the cloth before contact is made with the object ball, so that the cue ball can be made to screw back quite easily without any strength being used. In the diagram the cue and object balls

DIAGRAM 109.

Diagram 110 shows a screw in-off into a centre pocket, and the right and wrong way of playing the stroke. The ordinary player and even many very fair players, spot at the far end of the D in order to make the screw a little leas than ;when spotting away from the end of the D. When the kp-off is played in this way the object ball is given very bad direction, and good position for the next stroke depends on chance. The thin intersected line on the diagram gives an idea of the direction that may be given to the object ball when the in-off is played from the extreme left of the D. Of course this direction will vary as the contact between the balls varies, but it will always be bad and with some slight varia- tions of the position shown on the diagram, the object ball may catch the angles of a top pocket or a battik pocket, thus making the after posi- tion still more uncertain. If instead this screw in-off be played from the other position of the cue ball shown on the dia- gram, the object ball can be driven straight up and down the table or even thrown a little more to the centre of the table as shown by thick in- tersected line on the diagram. Thus splendid direction may be given the object ball and plenty of latitude exists for strength, for after the ball comes out of baulk (the strength, of the stroke drives it in and out of baulk) any stopping place up to about two feet from the top cushion means position for one or other of the four out-of-baulk pockets. Even if the stroke has been strong enough to bring the ball well down the table again after having struck the top cushion a second time, its direction will still be good. Nor is any very exact striking of the object ball neces- sary in order to leave good position. So long as the object ball is kept well away from the side of the table good position will generally be left. Played in this way this screw in-off may for ordinary players be a little more difficult than if played from the end of the D, but any player who wishes to improve his game must give up playing every stroke in the easiest possible way, otherwise he will always remain a hap-hazard player. .

With the cbject ball in the position shown on the diagram an in-off could be played into the left top pocket, but good players not excepting the best professionals never care to play top- pocket in-offs from baulk when good position can be easily retained by the medium of a centre pocket in-off.

A WORD TJ THE WISE.

The Examiner, Saturday June 3, 1905.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
are intended to be one inch apart, but the same stroke is quite possible when they are only three-quarters of an inch apart, and even less, ;but such a stroke requires excel). tionally good touch, and is quite beyond all except really good players. If the cue ball be placed nearer and nearer the object ball, a time arrives when a cannon by this particular kind of screw-back is no longer possible by any player, but a cannon is still possible by a totally ferent kind of screw when the balls are not more than an eighth or even a sixteenth of an incb apart. Such a stroke is really a marvellous one and not all professionals, and very few amateurs indeed, can get this Shot. On the other hand just a few players are wonderfully good at it Osborne, .the well-known Leicester professional is almost Derfect at it. I have seen, him set the cannon shown at A on Diagram 109 and the in- offs shown at B and C, time after time with the greatest of ease. ins screw-oacs with the cue ball so very near the object ball, is a very peculiar stroke in that the shot cannot possibly be made unless the cue, ball be struck with side ae well. as bottom. In fact, many players aver that such a stroke is a foul, even when the cue-ball screws right back. They argue that it must be a push, else why is the stroke impossible without side, when in all other screw-backs it is easier to screw without side than with side. In the stroke under discus- sion it is, however, side, and sidefonly, which pre- vents a push when the stroke has been well played. When two balls are almost touching, it is nearly impossible to push one through the other, if the cue-ball be struck with pignty of side and played full or nearly full on to the other. This may be proved by plac- ing two balls only one-sixteenth to one- eighth of an inch apart, and playing quite full on to the object ball with a smart, clean stroke, using a fair amount of strength and striking the cue-ball with plenty of side and without any bottom. It will then be found that the strong rotation imparted to the cue-ball prevents it from being pushed through the object ball, and though the object ball has been struck quite full the cue-ball is thrown off very squarely. To emphasise the difficulty of pushing the cue-ball through an object ball when the two balls are almost touching, and the cue-ball is struck with extreme side, the following experiment may be tried :—Spin a ball with the hand as strongly as possible, and in such a manner that after a second or two it ceases to move along the cloth though still rotating very rapidly. Place ano- ther ball almost touching it and right in front of it, and then strike the spinning ball on the side which would cause the same dire.ction of rota- tion in a stationary ball as alread3. shown in the moving ball. If the stroke be sharp and clean, it will be found that it is quite im- possible to push the spinning ball through the other ball. Instead, it will spring away very squarely. In the cannen ,shovvri at A the cue ball is thrown off very squarely ard then the reverse rotation on the, cue ball causes it to rush up the table, the path of the cue ball thus describing a very pronounced curve more or less as shown on the diagram. . . .

The best way to play these exceedingly close screws is to hold the cue as lightly as possible and to strike with plenty of dash, practically fhrowing the cue at the ball, aiming of course to hit the ball below the centre and with plenty of side. These screws, though most difficult strokes, are well worth learning by really good players with a penchant for fancy strokes, for they are perhaps the most telling of all the strokes that can bs. shown on a billiard table. More than that, such a stroke may conceivably be of use during a game.

THE TRANSPORTER BRIDGE.

AN IMPORTANT LINK BETWEEN LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE.

Great Day at Runcorn and Widnes.

Monday was a never-to-be•forgotten day in the memories of Runcorn and Widnes inhabitants, and one also of supreme importance to ;he counties of Lanca- shire and Cheffiire, for it marked the opening to the public of the new transporter bridge which spans the Mersey tvithin almost a stone's-throw of the famous London and North-Westeta. Railway bridge. GREAT SANKEY AFFAIRS.

The Agitation for Station Improvements.

RECREATION GROUND WANTED.

Upto the present, theagitation for improved booking arrangements at Great Sankey Station to obviate the dangers of the present system, has had no satisfa :tory result. The subject was again under discussion at the meeting of the Parish Council held in the Council Schools on Monday evening under the presidency of Mr. G. Thompson. There were also present :—Messrs. Taylor, Purness,Faircloughffirch,lliggs,and Joseph Smith(clerk). The Clerk read the following letter which he had received from Mr. James Pinion, manager for the Cheshire Lines Committee have had your letter of the 22nd March under consideration and have had some figures prepared in regard to the traffic. It would appear to me that theaccommodation at Sankey is ample and sufficient for the traffic, especially when I observe from the statement before referred to that the passenger user of the'station has fallen off considerably. This being so, I am sue you will agree with me that I connot con- sistently recommend my committee to incur any further expense by providing additional booking arrangements. Should, however, a substantial improvement in the passenger traffic be observed, I shall be happy to recon- sider the matter.

Mr. Furness, while thanking Mr. Pinion for his courteous letter, said it seemed to him that, it was absolutely the duty of a railway company to provide facilities for their passengers, whether the number be one or a thousand, in order to obviate any danger. The conditions existing at Sankey station wete highly dangerous to those making use of it. The falling off in traffic might, he believed, to some extent he ascribed to the danger which must deter g:ople from using the sattion as much as they otherwise would do. Then again, he thought if Mr. Pinion could SC 3 his way to reduce the return fare between Sankey and Warrington he would no doubt find that the Sankey returns would improve considerably in a short time. The Council would not be jastified in allowing the matter to drop quietly. buoy should let it be known that they intended taking the course they bad laid out for themselves in the interests of the general public. 'they should insist upon the railway company giving them what they asksi for, as it was only reason- able and, be believed, nothing beyond what the Board of Trade would require if they went into the matter. The Chairman agreed with Mr. Furness's remarks with reference to the return fares. A charge of five-pence to Warrington and back, was, he considered, very excessive; and it would be in the interests of the C.L.C. to reduce the fare. They all knew his opinions with regard to the risk that people incurred in booking and crossing the line to the opposite platform. tie adhered to every word he bad said, and he was prepared to go with the Council in any steps they might take to remedy the existing conditions. The worst feature of Mr. Pinion's letter was that he did not appaar to realize the eanger. Mr. Riggs: It appears to be a question of 2s d with the committee. *The Chairman: With us it is a question of the danger to the pissengers. He suggested that the Clerk should write to Mr. Pinion again, pointing out the desir- ability of a reduction of the return fare to Warrington andasking him to state definitely whether he considered the station was all it should be from the point of view of the safety of the passengers. Mr. Furness thought Mr. Pinion's position would debar him from expressing a cindid opinion. If he admitted that the station was dangerous he would bring down the censure of the Board of Trade. The Council had not to consider, whether the station was paying or not. The point Was: were' the conditions , such as the Board of Trade would demand and the general public reinired ? lie m iintaine I thu thay were not.

After further discussion it was decidod that the Clerk should again write to Mr. Pinion expressing regret that he could not see his way to recom need the improve- ment. and-pointing ow; that the Council intended to ' continue to agitate in the matter. Later the matter was further discussed in committee.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
ITECEEATION GROUND WANTED.

The Clerk read a petition signedTy- numerous re dents asking the Chairman to bring before the Council the great want of a recreation ground for the children of the district. Groat danger lay in their playing about the road as it was the main thoroughfare between two large cities, and the tra'fic was very heavy. Penketh and Bank Quay districts had both their recreation grounds and the petitioners hoped that through the influence of the Council, Sankey haveone too. The Chairman said a playground was very desirable but the difficulty would be to find a suitaiile piece of laud. The parish would also have to incur the expanse of fencing and laving it out. Mr. Taylor : The rates are high enough. It was *decided that the matter should remain in abey- ance for the present.

WELCOME NEWS TO R ITEP AY EMS. The Chairman said the overseers had felt justified in reducing the rates for the township by 2d. in the £ for the present half year. The Clerk remarked that Sinker, was the only parish that he knew of, whera the rates had bean reduced. AD CP lON AL POSTAL PAC [LIT IE REQUIRED. The Cnairtnan said that several ratepayers at the Great Sankey end of the district wortld like a Sunday evening collection of letters front the pillar boa near the church. At present there was no collection between Saturday night and Slonday. and the inhabitants were .were greatly inconvenienced. It was decided to apply to the Postmaster for the additional collection.

A RE3IGNATIOST. Owing to removal fro n the district Mr. F. Jefferson wrote intimating that he felt it his duty to resign his seat on the Nfanagement Committee of the Sankey schools. He thanked the members of the Council for the confidence they had placed in him. The Clerk was instructed to write expressing the Council's regret at Mr. Jefferson's removal. The Chairmln said he thounhe he would be carrying out the wishes of the Council in suggesting the na!TIA of Mr. Furness in succession to Mr. Jefferson on the School Niamagernent Committee. The vacericy had not yet been considered. The Council agreed with this course.

Cycling Gossip.

BY THE CLUBMAN.

The above sketch depicts a scene to be observed daily and hourly on all our roads. The speed of the car (which is cutting in and out) will certainly not be less than twenty, and may be as much as forty, miles an hour. The feelings of cyclists coming in the reverse direc- tion to the motor, and being shaved by feet and inches, can be better imagined than described.

A Giant and His Bicycle.

Perhaps the largest bicycle ever built_ was that recently

z;upplied by a Birminghana firm to the order of a Mr. O'Connor, who stands 'ft. 24 in. high. The size of frame is 34 in., the wheels 30 in., and the distance from saddle to pedal measures 45 in. Had Mr. O'Connor been a cyclist in the days of the tall bicycle, when short cranks were in vogue, he would have required a driving-wheel of 66 in. or 68 in. diatneter, What that would have meant can be judged from this old sketch of a 52-in. ordinary.

A Canted Pedal.

Dr. B. A. Lundie, of Edinburgh, has hit upon what he claims to be an improvement in the con- struction of pedals. It appears that when the feet are placed about G in. apart, a greater pressure is exerted on the outer portions of the soles than elsewhere. This, it is alleged, is sometimes the cause of corns on the feet. Anyhow, by raising or canting the outer parts of the pedal-rubbers—or iat-traps, as the case may be, it is sought by Dr. kindle to render pedalling more efficient and comfortable. It is very doubtful, however, if the innovation will meet with adoption in these days of narrow treads, as one effect would be to turn in the rider's knees, and thus encourage objectionable chafing against the top tube of the frame.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
Comfort in Cycling.

The lightness of the modern cycle has certainly greatly encouraged cycling among ladies, and variable speed gears are, perhaps, an even greater help. Especially to those who ride fully equipped roadsters for business and pleasure, not to mention Carrying luggage, has the variable speed gear Proved a boon. The questions of gearing and weight arc intimately associated, and should be considered jointly if comfort in riding is to be realized. A gear tolerably easy to propel under average conditions becomes a stiff task when hills abound or adverse winds are encountered. With- out a two-speed gear comfort can only he ensured by adopting a moderate gear in relation to the Weight of the cycle. Thus 60 in. on a heavy mount, or 70 in. on a very light one, may be cited as Moderate gears when a twO-speed hub is not employed. But when a two-speed hub is used our 60-in. gear can he increased to 70 in., and the 70-in. gear to 80 in., with a proportionate increale of Speed and enjoyment when riding on the level. And when we come to a hill we can bring in our lower gear, which means a reduction of 23 per cent., a reduction that enables us to ride hills we must Otherwise walk; and when we reach the summit recourse is again had to the high gear, with its 23 per cent. higher power and speed. Such increased gear is found well within the rider's capacity until the next rise is reached, when lower gear is used; nn.l so comfort, speed, a LILL power are all helped by the two-speed gear. Before deciding on a two-speed gear the rider should read the interesting booklets on . speed gears which are sent out free by the Eadie Manufacturing Co., of Redditch, and by the Hub Tye-Speed Gear Co., of Salford, Manchester.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
Side-Slip in Dust.

Much has been written about the dangers of hide-slip on muddy roads, but it is possible for a cyclist to ride for months or seasons before he

eriperiences a fall from his wheel slipping in dust. Nails of this kind most frequently occur on the 40ping sides or edges of roads during periods of drought, when a considerable depth of powdery Material accumulates on the hard surface of the highway. Quite another cause of side-slip, equally unpleasant to encounter, is indirectly due to dust ---thuswise: the wood or asphalte pavements of towns are often watered in summer along the margins Or gutters only. Traffic churns up a pasty raucr, and dry dust is subsequently blown over the danger-spot, giving it the aspect of dry road. The rider's astonishment is only equalled by his dismay When his wheel skids on the treacherously concealed surface.

SCIENCE NOTES AND GLEANINGS.

Iv alarm for telling the presence of illuminating gas or fire-damp in the atmosphere has been brought before the Academy of Sciences, Paris. It is really a balance of precision, which weighs the air under test against an equal quantity of normal air.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
POST-MORTEM HEART-BEAT.

Hearts of cold-blooded animals will beat for a comparatively long time after death or removal from the body (if kept cool and moist), because of powerful internal collections of nerves, known as ganglia, whose automatic impulses cause the regular contractions of the muscles. Similar ganglia exist in man and other warm-blooded animals, but their action is less prolonged. A turtle's heart, after removal, has been known to beat thirty-six or even forty-eight hours; twelvo or fourteen hours is a common record.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
A RADIO-ACTIVE METAL.

A mineral found in Ceylon possesses, according to Professor Dunstan and Mr. G. S. Blake, radio- active properties. Its crystals are more or less cubical in shape, black, rather lighter than silver, and about as hard as rock crystal. As the mineral, called Thorianite, contains 80 per cent, of thorium oxide, it has considerable commercial value. The remainder is chiefly uranium oxide, with a little of two other rare earths and lead. Thorianite is helium radio-active, and when dissolved evolves helium, into which one of the emanations from radium, now almost priceless, has been proved to change.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
SAHARAN VOLCANOES.

Another feature of the remoter history of the Sahara has been revealed. We know that bra desert abounds with traces of an ancient system of irrigation, which the late Cardinal Lavigeria con- templated restoring. We also know now, on the authority of the eminent explorers Foureau and Geatil, that the desert is thickly studded with extinct volcanoes, ancient and modern. The basaltic remains of the Grand Erb and the many basaltic peaks of the Adrar are mentioned. But the most remarkable case is that of the Air district, where over a territory of ninety-three miles in extent many volcanic peaks are met with, recent enough to shew the craters well preserved.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
VARYING SIGHT IN THE TWO EYES.

It has often been mentioned that some people have for years had one eye blind without knowing it. Mr. Aitchison, a well-known optician, says he has found no such case during his testing of more than 100,000 persons. Many sufferers —and many, too, who think their sight perfect— have a greater visual acuity in one eye than in the other. With regard to the respective power of the right and left eye, Mr. Aitchison finds that a person occupied in writing all day has, as a rule, stronger vision in the left. Writing with the right hand, and his left arm resting on the table, his left eye is nearer his work, and its vision is more concentrated. Mr. Aitchisoa holds that our race will never become so shortsighted as the German whilst football, cricket, tennis, ,hockey, &c., are encouraged in our public schools.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW TO FERTILISE SWAMP LANDS.

Black swamp lands, seemingly rich in plant food, are often unproductive after being reclaimed a few years. Many of them contain large quantities of nitrogen and plenty of phosphoric acid, but are lacking in potash. The potash which was formerly in the peat and vegetable matter, which go to make up these lands, has been largely washed out in the drainage waters. Experiments in certain soils shew that the addition of potash has brought marvellous returns, and is the only element needed in such soils to produce profitable crops. In one instance the application of a ton per acre gave an increase of over thirty bushels of corn the first year, and in eleven years the gain over the natural soil amounted to nearly 600 bushels per acre. One test shewed that without potash no grain was produced, but 2001 b. muriate of potash per acre gave thirty-six bushels of corn while potash with nitrogen and phosphoric acid produced a yield of sixty bushels.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
FIREFLY v. ELECTRIC LIG lIT.

Concluding a course of lectures at the Royal Institution on "Flame," Sir James Dewar exhibited a tabalar statement shewing the small amount of light, compared with the energy expended, which the best of human appliances yield, and how utterly man is beaten by the firefly. The figures stand somewhat as follow: •

Energy, Candle ... 2 ... 98 Oil 2 ... 98 Coal gas 2 ... 98 Incandescent lamp ... 3 ... 97 Arc lamp Magnesium lamp ... 15 ... 85 Cuban firefly ... 99 ... 1 In the economy of light the little phosphorescent beetle of the Eastern or Western Archipelagos is easily first. If we could produce light as efficiently, either by gas or electricity, millions of tons of coal would be saved in Britain every yesr.

WHAT BECOMES OF THE MICROBES?

As most, if not all, of the more impor ant diseases are to-day being ascribed to microbes, the question naturally arises, when a person dies of contagious disease, as to what becomes of the germs that caused his death, and that abound in his body? The subject has been largely investigated by Dr. Klein. of Germany, who has buried the bodies of infected animals for stated periods, and then examined them fcr grrm life. The doe or concludes as follows: The bacillus prodiyosus and the se. phylococcus curses are found living in dead bodies twenty-eight days after interment. Lenges stay under ground kills thorn; after six weeks no culture develops. The bacillus of cholera lives nineteen days, but does not preserve its reproductive powers after eighteen days. The resistance of Ebettli's bacillus (typhoid) is nearly the same. 'I he germ of plague is always alive after seventeen days of burial, but not after three weeks. The bacillus of consumption does not survive the person it has killed.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
TO PREVENT RAILS FROM SPREADING.

An ingenious device, designed by Mr. G. W. North, traffic manager of the North Wales Narrow Gauge Railway, the scope of which is to prevent railroad metals spreading under the influence of atmostheric heat, and thereby eliminate accidents attributable to this cause, comprises a fiat iron bar which is attached over the ties but under the metals. There are two iron struts fitting outside the rails and under the .heulder forged on the bar. The struts are at an angle, and constructed so as to tit the bottom outside flange of the rail. Inside the rails there is a clip piece of wrought iron fitting closely over the inner side flanges, having struts on both inner and outer rails, so that on any curve the railroad cannot spread outward or the gauge become altered. The danger on railways, especially on curves, is the tendency of the rails to spread at the head, thus widening. the distance between the rails, and unless constant vigilance is maintained at well points a derailment is bound to occur 6o:mer or later.

VISION OF DEEP SEA FAUNA.

As we go further and further down into the great depths of the see, which may reach 28,0001 t. to 30,000 it., the temperature diminishes and tends to approach zero, whilst the light , grows rapidly li ebler, until I elow 1,2130 ft., remarks 7Mr. E. B6richard in Cc 51,105, it no longer penetrates; there is eternal darkness. Plants, not heing able to grow without light, disappear at this level; as a first consequence of lite in tho darkness all animal life found below it must be carniVorous: there also result important modifications in the orgzans of vision. By the side ,of creatures deprived ft Bence of sight we have others provided with huge organs of vision. The crustacean chrycophorus, carnivorous swimmer, with enormous eyeA, has luminous organs !attached to these: two others mantent the upper part of the thorax, and still another pair the ventral surface. The structure of these latter organs is complex; they include essen- tially a reflector, shaped like a horseshoe, and a lens to concentra m. Ibo light. The organ that is attached to the eye is lees complex, having no lens, but only a parabolic reflector. Among the tiShvE these special organs are not less minien.us and varied; in the stumiades there is a double set of luminous organs, cute set being ly the side of the lishing-filaments. The function or tim organs that accompany the eyes is to illuminate the surround. iug space tl unthle tho animal to perceive ita prey.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.! NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication most in ell cases send their IIiiMPS and addresses to the Editor, as a guarantee of good faith.

The English Sunday Closing Bill. Sir —I read, with mingled feelings," The Manchester Guardian's" report of the debate on this Bill, la the House of Commons on Friday last, and the very first thing that struck me was the paucity of the members present. Although the measure was of the highest im- portance, only 222, or about one third of the whole of the representatives of the people, found it convenient to grace the proceedings with their presence. There is nothing very remarkable in this however, for it is well- known that whenever any attempt is made in Parlia- ment to uphold the honour of God and thereby promote the vital interests of the nation,our members, to a large ' extent, keep away from the House, feeling,probably, an aversion to such work. Of the 222 members who put in an appearance on the present occasion, only 108 voted for the Bill and 114 against it, the measure being thus thrown oat by a majority of six. And the result need not surprise us when we consider the low tone of the speeches that were made for and against the Bill. The advocates for Sunday Closing based their action mainly on the good results that such a measure had produced in Scotland, Ireland and Wales, while their opponents condemned the shutting of the houses as an infringe- ment of theliberty of the workingman. The publican and his assistants mast lose•their rest day and violate the law of God at the same time in order that this man may enjoy his beer fresh and sparkling from the tap! I hope and believe that the great bulk of our working men would indignantly repudiate such an abominable doctrine as this! It is well known to the policeand to the publicans themselves that it is not the sober and intelligent portion of the working clas es who frequent the top rooms on Sundays and create dis- order in the streets. But no Balfonr, Campbell Banner- man, or other stalwart politician appeared on the scene to lift the discussion out of the miserable rut in which it floundered, and remind his hearers that there is a law of God, read in the churches every Sunday, which pro- kibits work.of all kinds -from being done on the seventh day. And did not the debaters know that, to the credit and advantage of Englishmen,thislaw was very generally obeyed, the brewers and politicans being amongst the most prominent exceptions. And why should these exceptions be tolerated? there something so saprernely good in intosicatingliquors that their manu- facture and sale on Sundays should be permitted to override the law of God! The men who carry on the occupation that are necessary for the anstainment and comfort of human life are glad when Sunday comes round that they may have a whole day's rest from their labours. And nobody asks or compels them to forego that inestimable and heaven appointed boon. But the' brewers and publicans whose wares stimulate and minister to our lowest and most degrading passions are authorised by the Government of a Christian country to absorb the whole week in their operations ! This is a national sin which is slowly but surely sapping the very foundations of society, and preparing the way for a terrible end. And my conviction is that, next to our legislators who are the prime offenders in the matter, the ministers of nearly all denominations are responsible for it. They know that drinking, but especially Sunday drinking, dishononrs God, baffles their efforts to improve the conditions of society and entails an incalculable amount of suffering and sorrow upon all classes of .neople. But with folded hands and wondering eyes they look on the scene, and make no effort, worthy of the name, to stay the plague.—Yours truly, May, 1905.

lONA.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
The Toleration of Churchmen.

Sir,—l think, judging from the tone of the discussion upon the observance of Holy days, that there is a dis- position to give the whole subject a fair consideration. If I have spoken of the importance of the Education Committee keeping within their own sphere in matters of this kind, it is not from any desire to reflect in any invidious way upon the action of the committee, as I am well aware of the peculiar difficulties that such questions present. When it is said that 'the authority of the Education Committee must be supreme," I take it that what is meant is that it is supreme within the province assigned to it by the Act of Parliament which called it into being, an Act which must itself be construed in a way not to violateany of the great principles which are now part of the constitution of the couatry. The attempt on the part of the committee to assume anything of the nature of spiritual jurisdiction would be a distinct violation of a prin- ciple which is founded in the Common Law. Parlia- ment has also itself by distinct enactment laid down in the Toleration Acts the principle that everybody is permitted to believe and worship as they please ; and it is no use any body or any person trying to get behind that principle. Parliament in adopting this principle virtually abdicated for itself all spiritual juris- diction, in other words it surrendered its right to interfere with the inner government of any religious body so long as such body did nothing illegal. For example Parliament could not now— mean could not consistently attempt—to make the bye- laws of the Wesleyan Conference or any other similar religious body. Loosely speaking we are apt to say that Parliament can do anything. It would be much nearer the truth to say that Parliament can attempt anything ; and if it attempted anything unwise it might end in failure. For example it might attempt to make rules regulating the practice of medicine, in a way the medical authorities did not approve. It is needless to say that any such attempt at legislation would be perfectly futile. Here comes in the application of the old legal maxim enilibei in arta sea peril() credenduat est. Parliament itself must keep within the bounds of reason and within the limits of the principles it has itself deliberately adopted; and Committees acting under the authority of Parlia- ment must be similarly careful to keep within their sphere ; otherwise they may find themselves spending the ratepayers' money in unnecessary law suits. It can- not be too strongly impressed upon your readers that everyone in this country is now free to believe and worship as he pleases ; that it is an accepted principle of the constitution ; and it is no use anyone attempting under any pretext to evade its operation. And this of course carries with it the right of the parent to have his children educated in the faith that he himself professes —a privilege that is not affected by the fact that a large part of the expense of Education is now thrown upon the rates. If our liberties were to be circumscribed afresh every time there was a new incidence of taxa- tion we should very soon have no liberties at all. If people who send their representatives to the Town Council empowered to levy rates, think themselves at liberty to refuse to pay them when they have been levied, then anything like popular representative government is at an end. And with regard to the privilege of which I speak, namely the free exercise of oar religion, it belongs to the smallest religious body in the country whose contribution to the exchequer is of the smallest as well as to the larger bodies whose con- tribution is large in proportion. The principle is clear, and it is not to be obstructed by certain people who complain that it compels them to pay an extra ss. a Tear in the shape of rates. I should have thought that ihe piisssive resisters, judging from their professions, would have willingly paid a few shillings extra a year to further the principle of toleration rather than spend money in obstructing it. Nor is the principle of toleration satisfied by adopting a view of education acceptable only to the secular party, and expecting everybody else to fall in with that view. It is not to be satisfied with anything less than every loyal subject of the Sovereign being perfectly free to act and think for himself and his family in all matters of religious conviction. No religious body has a right to coerce another, and a man with secular views has no more right to expect people with different opinions to fall in with his methods, than a Teligions man has to expect the secular man to fall in with his methods. The principle I Ray is clear, though I admit the practical application of the principle may not always be so easy; but surety difficulties that have been sur- mounted io nearly all our School Boards may be expected to disappear even with ourselves if considered in a reasonable spirit, and only the friends of toleration co-operate. I notice that Mr. Monks in his letter—with much of which Tam quite in agreernent—refers to " Ecclesiastics who base their refusal to comply with the requirements of the Education Committee on the higher authority of the Church." I presume the members of the Church of Rome are here intended, as they are the only eccle- siastics that I know of that do not limit the authority of the Church to matters spiritual. It 4, however, a curious thing that these peculiar claims of the Roman Church have actually received a certain amount of recognition from the Warrington Education Committee. • I am &c.,

E. C. E. CARLETON.

Cricket.

Warrington enjoyed a relaxation from League cricket on Saturday when they engaged in a friendly match with Bollington at Arpley. The weather con- ditions were ideal for the summer sport, and an interesting afternoon's cricket was witnessed. The result was a draw, but no fault could be found with the Warringtonians who acquitted themselves exceed- ingly well. Batting first, they had lost only two wickets for the splendid total of 215 when the innings was declared. The visitors were also in a scoring mood and had responded with 104 with only 4 wickets down when the time limit was reached.

Mason, the Warrington professional, was the hero of the match. His innings was a really splendid dis- play, and realized 148 —beingonly three short of the i record for the club which s held by H. Tinsley, a former professional. Mason was at the wicket two hours, and his hard and clean hitting gave delight to the Warrington spectators. His chief hits included nineteen tours, six threes, and twelve two's—a notable performance.

P. B. Hawtin also exhibited fine form with the bat, and was not out with 54 to his credit. 114 made ,four bonndary strokes, and his display although suffering by comparison with Mason's, was, nevertheless praise- worthy. Left with nearly two hours in which to save the match, Bollington made good use of their opportuni- ties V. Hope made a long stand and was mainly res- ponsible for the draw, his score being 40 L. Haigh lalso gave valuable help, knocking up 35.

' Warrington 2nd were easily victorious at Bollington. The home team could do very little against the de- liveries ofit.ennett and Lowe who captured five wickets for 21 runs and five for 50 respectively. F. Pierpoint was foremost with the bat and compiled 57 in vigorous style, while E. E. Woods gave a meritorious display for his 41.

Sankey Wire Mills disposed of Winstanley Park in surprisingly easy fashion. Declaring their innings at 1115 for the loss of 7 wickets the Wire Mills applied themselves in no half-hearted style to the task of dismissing their opponents, and succeeded in getting them all out for 56. Woodcock (33) was the top scorer for the victors, while Burton bowled with deadly effect, taking four wickets for 16 runs.

Sankey Wire Mills second also registered a victory at the expense of St. Paul's. There was no tall scor- ing on either side, W. Lawless (20) for St. Paul's accomplishing tie best performance. ,

There was an exciting contest, between Cheshire Lines Engineers and fiewsey Wesleyans, and in the end the Engineers triumphed by .a margin of 3 runs. Duckworth batted splenedly for the winners,, his inn- ings producing 48 (not out). The same player also bowled in grand style, capturing 4 wickets for 6 runs, while Deeley took 4 for 15.

The Engineers' junior eleven were entertained at radgate Cottage Homes, and ddeated a team composed of officers arta:lays by 11 runs. The players sat down to a capital tea, and a thoroughly enjoyable afternoon and evening were spent.

The secondary School eleven have acquired a suit- able field at Padgate, but the.pitoh has not quite got into working order. The consequence was that the match with Widnes Juniors on Saturday was chiefly notable fur the great array of "ducks." The Secondary School triumphed thanks in a great measure to the bowling of Ifinards who captured five wickets for 4 runs.

st. James's accounted for Walkers Brewery 2nd by a margin of 10, the Rev. D. R. Davies being their highest scorer with 14.

Mersey-street defeated Fletcher RuaselFs, the match being remarkable for low scoring. Not a single player attained double figures.

Stretton and Appleton may account -themselves fortunate in not being defeated by Arley who were much the superior team but had not Oita time to capture their opponents' last wicket. J. V. Smith batted in grand style for 59.

• Grappenhall beat Lymm very easily the fine batting and the play by Wheen (46) being the feature of the match.

Bowling.

Fearnhead are going strong for the championship of the Warrington and District League, as with four matches played they have captured the maximum number of points k the league table. Evidently the struggle is to lie between them and the Bull's Head team who with three matches 'played have recorded six points. Though it would not appear so from the league table the Church-street men hold a big Advan- tage having played two matches away from home against one played on a foreign green by the club which at present occupies the lead. '

On Saturday Fearnhead had an easy task, but one which did not prove as simple as many members of the club anticipated. It i,s confidently expected by them that they would improve their scoring average by obtaining al; balance of 100 points,as the result of the match with Penketh Park, and I was privately and seriously informed that that would be the extent of the defeat of the wooden-spoonists. As I anticipated, however, Penketh Park were smarting under the reverses of the previous Saturday, and although they did not win—it was not expected that they would they did the next best thing and played up in a manner which surprised their opponents, scoring within CO of the Fearnhead totd.

The Horse and Jockey team gave an extremely poor account of themselves on the Bulls Head Green. They are by no means an indifferent lot of trundlers and to lose all but two of the twelve games played speaks either badly for them or well for the winners. lam inclined to think the latter is the case. The Bull's Head players are undeniably a superior combination and though it is still early in the season I venture to assert that when bowling finishes for this year the Church-street men will figure on the top of the league table.

Last year the struggle lay between them and the Cemetery men but early this season the champions were in indifferent form. On Saturday they allowed 3 promise of redeeming their reputation by defeating the Horse and Jockey Club by 33 points The match was not without its surprise as at one state of the game the winners were in arrears. As has been the case on many occasions however, the rearguard asserted itself and pulled the match out of the fire,

In addition to the Horse and Jockey players the Sankey Club also fell victims to the Bull's Head men on the Church-street green, being defeated, by 83 points. Of their players five only reached double figures, two alone successfully accounting for their opponents.

The question has been raised as to the advisability of presenting a consolation prize for clubs defeated in the 'first", round of the competition. The com- mittee has had the matter before them but as it has to be considered by a general meeting of the members it has been referred to the annual meeting of the Associa- tion, and probably next season some such scheme will be inaugurated.

Cycling The weather continues to favour the indulgence in out-door exercise and:as a result wheelers have been greatly in evidence. On Saturday about 20 members of the Warrington Cycling Club had an enjoyable run to Cranage, returning via Knutsford. On Tuesday a like number turned oat for the run toHalton Castle. A large section of the riders went nia Widnes with the object of crossing to • Runcorn by means of the new transporter bridge. On account of s breakdown in the machinery of the bridge they were however .doomed to disappointment and had to proceed to Runcorn by means of the old bridge. To-day tbe club ran is to Oulton Park and on Tuesday, Warburton will be visited.

Three youths. at Kersall Hill have been amusing themselves by placing tin-tacks on the highway and witnessing the discomfiture of cyclists and motorists whose tyres have been damaged in consequence. The police abruptly checked their little pranks by bringing them before the magistrates, and at Eddishury Petty Sessions on Monday they were mulcted in the costs. If the penalty has a deterrent effect, the police will have done something to earn the gratitude of the many wheelers who seek enjoyment in that part af Cheshire.
