The Examiner, Saturday, June 3, 1905.

MARRIAGE OF MR. S. TAYLOR, J.P. District Councillors and Guardians Entertained.

The members of- the Runcorn Rural District Council and Board of Guardians spent a highly enjoyable after- noon on Tuesday, when they were entertained at Dutton iby Mr. S. Taylor, Runcorn, n celebration of his recent marriage. Luncheon was served in the Board room at the Workhouse, some sixty gentlemen sitting down toan excellent repast, the catering of the Winmarleigh Can, Warrington, giving every satisfaction. After luncheon the time was pleasantly spent in speech- making and conviviality. The usual loyal toasts were honoured on the proposition of the Chairman.

===== 0002601_19050603_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

In proposing this toast Mr. Taylor said he had been a member of the Board.for many years and beet:mid assure them it had always been a pleasure to him to have been connected with the Board. Every question which had been brought before the Board had had careful con- sideration and been received with deep interest, especially questions affecting some unfortunate creatures who could not help themselves. The sympathy which had been shown in such cases had siv en him extreme pleasure. He hoped they would continue in the future as they had done in the past. They were sent there to guard the interest of the poor and he was sure it was a pleasure to them to do their duty. (Applause.) He coupled with the toast the names et the chair:lsm and vice-chairman. Mr. Norman was a most impartial chaii man and one who did not try to thrust his opinion down the throats of the other members of the Board. Mr. Baxter was an old member of the Board and he was sure everyone appreciated his work in connection with the Board. (Hear, hear.) He asked them to drink to the prosperity of the Board. The toast was heartily drunk. Mr. Norman in responding said he considered its high calling to represent the poor. He believed every mem- ber of the Board sympathised with the deserving cases which were brought before their notice. To some extent however their hands were tied but wherever they could stretch a point a little they did so. (Hear, hear.) He felt little credit was due to him for the manner in which the business was conducted but that I the credit was due to every member of the Board for tha support which they gave him. Death had visited their midst only recently and he hoped in the future that such visitations would be few and far between, but ,he hoped they would so order their lives that when the time did come they would be able to say they had done their duty. (Applause.) Mr. Baxter in returning thanks remarked that it I would be impossible to Bad a more popular man than their host and he was sure they hoped Mr. Taylor would live long to enjoy the company of Mrs. Taylor. I (Applause.) THE RURAL DISTRICT COIINCIL.

Mr. Taylor submitted the toast of the Rural District Council. He had had no experience of the work of the Council but from his knowledge of the work of the Board of Guardians, he was sure he was right when he said that the work of the Council was well carried out. He coupled with the toast the names of Mr. Woodward and Mr. Pilling.

The toast ha;ing been honoured, Mr. Woodward responded. Throughout the whole of Cheshire he did not think there was a single District Council more thorough in its work, and he did not think, if the Council had had the power of restrict- ing some expenses which had been incurred in recent years, that the public would be lamenting that expendi- ture as they were doing. He hoped they would continue to carry out the work as efficiently as they had done in the past. (Applause.) Mr. Pilling also briefly responded. The way the busi- ness was conducted by the District Council would, he thought, compare favourably with the conduct of busi- ness in any other part of the country. (Hear, hear)

===== 0002601_19050603_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GUESTS.

The toast was drunk on the proposition of Mr. Taylor, who coupled with it the name of Mr. P. J. Norman. Mr. Norman in responding said it had been a proud tradition of their family that they had always been con- nected with the work of Boards of Guardians, and he is pleased to think that his brother was so ably carrying out that tradition. He was sure they all wished Mr. Taylor long life and happiness and hoped that the con- cluding years of his life would be marked by calm and not by tempest. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19050603_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OFFICIALS.

The health of the officials was next drunk. In sub- mitting the toast Mr. Taylor spoke in high terms of the work of those gentlemen. In responding, Nit. G. F. Ashton gave some interest- ing statistics with regard to the Union. The greatest number of in-door paupers in one half-year, deal with by the Union was 245, and the least number 207—the latter being the number of paupers resident in the Workhouse during the past half-year. (Applause.) In out-relief the highest number dealt with was 1,026, and the lowest 738, the number last half-year being 768. The highest cost of inside paupers was £893, whereas last half-year it was £892. The greatest amount ever spent on out-relief in one half-year was £1,843. last half-year the sum spent is £1,638. Those figures were pleasing in view of the distress with the country. Last year the Board had been approached with regard to the establishment of relief works, but it was happily not found necessary to take such a step. Some remarks had been made about the cost of relief in Runcorn. In 1395 the sum spent was £2,410 in 1900 £2,125 and last year £2,025. People had complained about the poor tete being too high. Of that sum is. 3d. in the £ went to County expenses, sid. for education—(" slime ")—and 4,1 d. went to the poor. (Armlnuse.) Drs. Adams and McDoodle also responded. TH F: HOST. Mr. A. R. Normal, in submitting the heslth of Mr. S. Taylor, said they highly appreci ited Mr. Taylor's kind- ness on all occasions, and wish3d him and his wife long life and every happiness. (Applause). Mr. Woodward. Mr. Wright, and Mr. Spruce spoke to the toast, which was received with musical honour:. Mr. Taylor responded in a brief and humorous Speech, and shortly afterwards the proceedings concluded with the singing of the National Anthem. During tho afternoon songs were given by Mr. Smith and Mr. Cook,aod a humorous recitation by Mr. Hallows.

Richari Roberts, the old man who was discovered last week, atter twelve years search, to be the next of of kin and belt to a large fortune, alleged to exceed a million pounds, left by his uncle at Malvern Wells, died yesterday at Durham after two days' illness. LIBERAL ACTIVITY.

WARD COMMITTEES ENTERTAINED AT THE CLUB.

Mr. Howes Replies to Mr. /R. Pierooint M.P.

The enthusiasm of local Liberals was again shown on 1 Saturday when the Bewsey and Orford Ward Committees were entertained to tea at the club. An excellent repast was served. The subsequent proceedings were of an enjoyable character, speech-making being pleasingly interspersed with song. Mr. Owen presided and there were also present Councillors P. Peacock, and Wilkin- son? Messrs. J. Howes, J. A. J. Morton, and J. Howes, junior. Songs were given by Messrs. Tynan and Walker and Mr. E. A. Sanders pleased the audience with a number of excellent hnmourous recitations. Mr. J. Howes, jun., acted as accompanist. The CHAIRMAN remarked that the time was not far distant when they would have to fight. In Mr. Crosfleld they had a splendid prospective candidate and one of whom they need not be ashamed. (Applause.) In his opinion Mr. Crosfleld was fully conscious of the require- ments of the workingman. At the present time we heard a great deal of labour representation, but to his mind, and he had had a few years experience the Liberal_ policy was the best for the working- men. He had found that out from his own experience. (Applause.) The Liberal party were a Progressive party and meant to go ahead, and the views of any Liberal candidate were bound to fall in with the views of the Labour party. When the time arrived he hoped they would stand shoulder to shoulder as they had done in the past, and he did not think Mr. Crosfleld would be far behind. (Applause.) Councillor P. PEACOCK did not think thatthere would be an election,tiais year. He was of the opinion that the present Government would stick to their salaries for I another year, as Mr. Balfour and his relations knew perfectly;well that after the general election they would be out a office. (Applause). Mr. HowEs at the outset of a brief address explained that Mr. Crosfleld would have been present bad be not been detained in London on business. The Liberal party in Warrington, he continued, as a fighting force appeared to be in excellent trim and so far as he had been able to ascertain the Ward- Committees were not only numerous but seemed to be earnest and full of enthusiasm and zeal. If they had enthusiasm and unity they could win the seat at the next election. (Applause.) The responsibility would rest upon every one's shoulders. It was not going to be a one man show. They had an excellent prospective candidate but the best of candidates could not win by himself. He (Mr. Howes) would be the political agent but it is not the election agent who would win. To claim the victory it required the intelligent work of energetic committees, such as were present that night. At the next election there would be a great many seats won for the Liberal party but no seat was more important than Warrington. The fact that the electorate of the town was composed mostly of workingmen would cause their friends in London to look anxiously towards Warrington. It was expected that Lancashire would go strong as DO county in the whole country would suffer more than Lancaahire by the adoption of Mr. Chamber- lain's proposals. Therefore the Liberal party were looking to Lancashire for strong support and it would be a sad thing if every other district returned a Liberal and Warrington were to he left in the cold. He would not talk about being left in the cold as the only ques- tion was as to what the Liberal majority would be. (Applause.) He noticed that Mr. R. Pierpoint,lVLP., speaking about the fiscal question at a Conservative gathering on Monday evening, said "he confessed that if he wanted advice on matters of manufacture, he would ntinitely rather have the opinions of half-a-dozen of the ierninent manufacturers of Warrington than he would have the opinion of an equal nnmber of young gentle- men who belonged to the aristocratic families and who had no actual connection with business or commerce." No better argument could be used for sending Mr. Pierpoint to the right-about (Applause). Mr. Pierpoint was not connected with business, and he informed the electors that the best thing they could do was to send a business man to the House of Commons to deal with the fiscal question. (Applause.) "As his -hearers were aware," continued Mr. Pierpoint, "there were difficulties in the way under the present arrangements of getting their manufactured goods into foreign countries, owing to the high walls of tariff which were erected against them." To-day we ' had a greater foreign trade than any other country in the world in proportion to our population. We were now sending out a larger amount than any other nation, and we had a perfect right to record that fact to our being free traders. (Applause.) Before the introduction of Free Trade, wages in this country were low and prices were high where as since the application of the modern principle of free trade the prosperity of the people had increased and they had enjoyed far better times than under the old system. If free trade were wrong and protection right it followed that other countries would be in a better position than we were, and until Mr. Chamberlain could point to a single country in the world which was bet ter than we were we would be very foolish to adopt his policy. (Applause). Mr. Pierpoint proceeded to give figures as to the number of workingmen out of employment in England as compared with the number in Germany. He would not accuse Mr. Per- point of wilfully intending to deceive his hearers but say he did not know the real facts. Mr. Pierpoint proved his statement by giving trade union statistics but neglected to point out that while in this country we had an enormous number of trade unions in Germany there were very few organized trades. If Mr. Pierpoint would only take the blue book issued by the present government, he would find that the percent- age ofl unemployed in England was lower than in any protected country in the world. (Applause.) Mr. Pierpoint proceeded to refer to the Aliens Bill. It was a curious thing that the great Tory Government with their immense majority accused the weak Liberal party of being able to wreck their bills. The truth of the matter was that it was never the intention of the Con- servative Government to carry their original bill which like the present one was merely a bit of window dress- ing. (Applause.) The position of the Liberal party was clear. The Liberal members in the House of Commons had always expressed their readiness to help the Tories to I Pass a bill to exclude from this country criminal and diseased sliens,but what they insisted upon was that the I port should not be closed against the alien refugee who was fleeing from political injustice In his own land. No Bill framed on those lines would receive the support of It was not, however, tnheecesLisbareyrall, op aprats sy . a(Abipinilatuseo e./c ludo the criminal and diseased that because the law at present evoked ports to such aliens. Moreover country was very small as compared with the total tuhae to nunmlobseer our who remained in this aliens who came here. If Aliens had been excluded years ago a great many industries would not have existed in this country. Mr. Pierpoint asked why we objected to Aliens and answered his own question by saying "because they lowered the wages of our own people, undersold our labour, not so much in the skilled trades as in common labour, worked longer hours than our own people and were content to live on simple food which was below our standard." What could be said in reply to a statement like that comitig from a member of a party who had sanctioned the introduction of Chinese Labour into South Africa. If Aliens were to be excluded from England because they lowered wages why should they not be excluded frord South Africa? If the Government were in favour of the introduction of aliens into South Africa in otcler to increase the wealth of a few alien millionaires, and to cheapen the labour ut that colony. what argument had they against the introduction of aliens into other parts of the empire. The Aliens Bill was the greatest fraud by a fraudulent Government, whose leader declined to give a clear answer to a clear question, and was responsible for a disgraceful scene which had lowered the prestige of our name, and who would drag the honour of England in the gutter unless he was speedily dismissed from office. (Applause.) Mr. Pierpoint went on to refer to our foreign policy and said We have been on the verge of war and one trembled to think what would have taken place if the management of foreign affairs had been placed in inexperienced hands." That was said by the member of a party whose leader in the middle of a great crisis In the South African war went to Man- chester and said the man in the street knew more about the matter than he did. There never was a time when the foreign affairs of England were so badly managed as they had been by the present administration. (Applause.) Those utterances by Mr. Pierpoint would be used as tests aglinst that gentleman who was utterly incapable of representing the constitn- ency and who said that a business man was more to be depended upon. (Applause.) Councillor WILKINSON spoke strongly in favour of free trade, contrasting the position of the labourer in England with the labourer in Germany. .In England he explained the poorest paid labourer was the agricul- tural workman who received 17s. per week. In eidrewtuitrnhe a d Germany the workman had to keep himself and his family on 25 per cent less. (Applause.) SEVERELY BITTEN BY AN OTTEft. The Carlisle Otter Hounds had one of the finest day's sport ever experienced with the pack on the Ribble on Wednesday. An otter was located on the Hodder, and for upwards of half an hour it was in the open, with the hounds in full cry after it, both up and down the stream. Several times it sought refuge, and after a terrific struggle with the terriers, had to be dug out, one gentle- man being severely bitten in the hand in the operation. The otter then gave a run down stream, and before it was killed the hunt had lasted three hours. The otter weighed 121 b.

AN ADVENTUROUS TRIP.

A STORY OF THE NEW BRIDGE.

[By Thos. Saunders.]

The noise and bustle attendant on the opening of the Transporter Bridge had died away. Even the crowds that had flocked to the heights of the neighbouring railway bridge, to look upon the car as it journeyed to and fro suspended in mid-air, had gradually lessened until now you might count them at a glance. The man who had not made the journey across the new bridge was a rarity ; and even among women those who could not boast a similar experience were only the aged; and some timid spinsters. Somehow or other it leaked out at the club, that I had never made this passage. At once I became the object of the members' curiosity, and many were the shafts of ridicule hurled at me. _ _ _

I stood it well for a time, for I felt they would never understand me unless they stood in my shoes and possessed my inborn timid nature. But the sarcasm of certain members began to be unbearable, and I felt that I must either cross the new bridge or cease attending the club.

After one particular night of more than the average banter. I passed along the street to my home determined that another day should not dawn and me not have crossed the river in the car of the new Transporter.

I hastily swallowed my supper, then made my way towards the new bridge. It Ve as pitch dark, and, as T listened to the clock of the old church strike the hour of midnight, I could see the lights of the car as it once more neared our side of the river. Then I heard it slide smoothly into its berth, and my heart gave a huge bound for I knew that the time had arrived when I must set foot upon it if I intended to carry out my purpose. I was not in the frame of mind to hold intercourse with anyone, and at the sound of footsteps I slunk into a doorway, while the workmen who had just arrived by the car passed on their way home. Then I crept noise- lessly down the roadway of the bridge and took my seat. I sat in the furthermost corner, and sincerely wished that not another soul would occupy the com- partment that journey. The lights at the top of the towers were only turned half on, yet I could plainly see familiar objects of the landscape. To ray right was the monstre railway bridge, while to my left was the graceful spire of All Saints' Church. Then, as I brought my gaze to the in- side of the car again, I shuddered as I remembered where I sat. I would have given anything in exchange for an easy mind. As I thought of the dead in the neighbouring graveyard, I envied them their resting-place. Thus and thus were my thoughts when I saw the door of the car move cautiously along its sliding way. In another minute the figure of a woman had crept inside and taken the corner nearest the door. She was closely muffled, and even had I wished, I could not have discovered her identity. I resigned myself to fate, as I thought, and wished for ' the start to be made. I could bear the intrusion of this one quiet woman, but another would make me post- pone my journey to some future day. I was gradually working myself into a state of hysteria at the delay. How I wished that I had stayed until the moment in my hiding place in the doorway. Just then the muffled figure in the corner made a movement and partly uncovered her head. She was evidently greatly distressed, and nursed beneath her cloak a small bundle of humanity. My sympathy was aroused, for as I looked upon her, I saw tears fall upon the bundle she held closely to her breast. Whilst I wondered what was the cause of her trouble, I did not wish to pry into it, so turned my eyes away again. Perhaps an indifferent husband, who had caused her at this midnight hour to search for him in his drunken haunts, yet forced to return without him. I had always been opposed to drunkenness, but, as I dwelt upon the wrongdoing of this imaginary bad husband, I thought it would be a pleasure to see him sink beneath the black waters of the river. Had she appealed to me at that moment for help against him, I would willingly have walked off the car and gone in search of the neglectful spouse. But it was not to be: I must needs be content to silently sympathise and alternately cast furtive glances in her direction. How she hugged her wee bit of comfort to her, and kissed passionately the shawls that encircled its tiny head. She was unmindful of my presence, and even when I coughed as a hint of my , whereabouts, she took no further notice. Once I heard a little cry, as if in her affection for it she had held her charge too tightly. Instantly she was soothing the little sufferer by increased caresses, and] rocked it backwards and forwards. _ _

"Hush! hush! my little pet, I will not hurt you ; give me another kiss." And, bending lower, she kissed the bundle of shawls as if it held all the treasure the world contained for her. At that moment the signal was given to start, and we began to float noislessly over the waters of the river. As though the movement of the car reminded her of her surroundings, she looked across my way and our eyes met. She was a woman of over forty years. Once she had been a most beautiful creature, but lines of care were making sad havoc of a face made to bewitch. Again, I silently placed bad wishes upon the head of the unknown husband.

We were now passing quickly over the river, and were soon opposite one of the stone pillars of the railway bridge. I turned my eyes away from my solitary com- panion, for I thought that my persistent gaze might pain her. But as often happens, an unknown influence caused me to turn my eyes in her direction again. I was surprised to see her standing opposite the open window on the other side of the car. Her back was turned towards me, yet I could see that she was still engaged in kissing and dropping tears upon the wee creature she nursed so assiduously. A spell was upon rue, I could not take my eyes from the muffled form. In an instant, and when we were about fifty yards from the other side of the river, she raised the bundle high in her arms, and. before I could utter a word she had flung it through the open window into the river beneath.

I was horrified. I rose from my seat and then stood like one petrified. She was walking quietly back to her seat in the corner. This roused my blood to fever heat, and, quickly striding to where she sat, I stood before her and hissed out :

" You had woman ! You have drowned your baby." And at the sound of my own voice, I fancy I could hear the 1 ast gurgles of the drowning infant. Instead of answering me', she buried her face in her hands and wept copious floods of tears. Placing my hand upon her shoulder I spoke again. "Woman," I said, do you know what you have done ?"

She looked up as if that was the first time she had heard my voice. With a tone vibrating with sorrow, she said :

" She was sick and dying, and I could not boar to look upon her. She had been my only source of com- fort, and I could not see my darling die." My heart was strangely moved, for the woman before me had evidently acted as she thought for the best. Then there loomed up before my mind's eye,the horrible crime I had witnessed. I was in a dilemma. Should give this woman into custody, and thus add the " last straw ?" Or should I allow her to go free, and thus aid and abet a mnrderess ? At this moment the car glided into its berth and all was still'

I had reached the door with the intention of hastily leaving this atmosphere of crime, when, as I turned, I saw the woman sitting quietly in the corner as if she would make the return journey. Touch. ing the conductor lightly upon the shoulder, I whispered into his ear the story of the crime I had just witnessed. He stared at me as if he thought I had been drinking ; but when I pointed to the silent figure inside he evidently thought there might be something in what I had told him, for he pulled me into the shadow while I gave him further particulars. As .I conclude', he muttered to himself : .

" Well, well hardly been running a month when a woman makes my car the scene of a murder." Then turning to me he said : " You must stand by the door of the car to see she doesn't escape, while I bring the whole force of the law upon her head. Yes, I'll have her hung, that I will, the hussy," he continued, assum- ing the tone of a man of great importance. As he ran up the road to inform the police of the affair, I heard him call to the night watchman, that a child was in the river. .

Almost as quickly as it takes me to tell the story, I saw a little craft shoot from beneath the gangway of the bridge, and make for the route of the car. Then the forms of three! stalwart policemen appeared down the slope of the bridge. As they approached,the conductor stepped officiously in front of them, and, throwing open the door, ushered them into the presence of the woman. At tho sound of their footsteps she looked up, and, seeing the law personified, she was startled. When one of the constables told her she must consider herself in custody, she trembled in every limb.

PROTECTING THE CHILDREN. _ Work of the N.S.P.C.C. in Warrington and District. Further convincing evidence of the good work which the National Society for the Prevention of cruelty to children is doing in Warrington was afforded at the annual meeting of the local branch which was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall on Wednesday. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., had been announced to pre- side, but wired regretting his inability to attend. Mr. F. A. Frost was elected to the chair. There was a good attendance, chiefly of ladies. THE YEAR'S WORK. The Ho, SECRETARY (Mr. W. D. Jeans, junr.), pre- sented the annual report, which reported a decrease in the number of cases dealt with in the district during the year. The total number of cases investigated was 274, affecting 901 children. In 259 cases warnings were given (the majority of those cases being still under supervision) ; in 12 prosecution followed, and 3 were dropped, the complaint having been found to be unfounded. 1,745 visits of supervision were made during the year. There were at present about 400 houses under the Inspector's supervision. The Committee were pleased to be able to report a successful financial year. Although the amount collected showed a decrease of £24 Os. 101, the expenses of the branch have decreased also by £0 us. 7d., and there was a surplus which was devoted to the general objects of the Society in other parts of the country of £43 13s. The Children's League of Pity had, thanks to Miss Broadbent's management, been suc- cessful in obtaining £35 13s. 3d., as against £24 2s. Bd. last year. The Committee desired to point out that the money subscribed to this Society was not (except a small portion of it) given away in charity, but was spent in bringing the parents or guardians of neglected or suffer- ing children who could but would not do their duty to their offspring, to a sense of their duty and to compel them to feed, clothe, aad treat their children in a reasonable and proper manner. During the year Inspector Hackett had been promoted to Sheffield,and the committee, while regretting the departure of this excellent officer, were glad to be able to congratulate him ozi his promotion. An efficient substitute for him had been provided in Inspector Cox with whose work the committee were well satisfied. Assistance to the society was thankfully acknowledged to the police in all parts of the district. Schoolmasters, Attendance Officers and other public officials also were very good in giving information as to cases. The Society's Offices had during the year been removed to more commodious premises at 49, Bewsey-street, War- rington. The committee desired to remind subscribers and others that a bazaar would be held at Chester in the month of October 1905, with the object of raising money to pay off the debt on the principal offices of the society in Leicester-square London and to provide a reserve fund and working balance so as to enable the society's field of operations to be enlarged. Ccntributions might be sent to Mrs. Frost, Grapenhall Hall, Mrs. Rylands, Dingle Bank, Lymm, Mrs. George Dalian or Mrs. Munro, Latchford, or to Miss Pennington, Park-road, Newton- le-Willows. . .

The statement of accounts showed that during the• year £257 Is. 2d. was collected from all sources. The general contributions from Warrington amounted to £135 17s. 6d., while the League of Pity subscribed 1:27 10s. 9d. Lymm contributed £32 10s. ; Newton, £39 16s. 10d. ; while the sum paid direct to the Central Office was £8 2s. Gd. The report and statement of accounts were adopted. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. The CUM-I'OllN proposed the re-election of Sir Gilbert Greenall. Bart., as president of the branch, and Lady Greenall, Mr. IL Pierpoint, M.P., Mr. A. IL Lee, and Mr. W. Long as vice-presidents. Mr. Mclnnis seconded and the motion was carried unanimously. The General Committee was re-elected with the addition of the Rev. W. Bracecamp, Mrs. Seger Owen, Miss Litton, Mr. R. A. Naylor, Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, Mr. Herbert Woods, Mr. J. R. Young, and Mr. H. Lister- Kayo. The Executive Committee was elected as follows: Alderman Bennett, Miss Broadbent, Mr. G Dakin, Mr. Vere B. Davies, Mrs. James Fairclough, Mrs. Frost, Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes, Mr. A. H. Lee, Mrs. Long. 'Mrs. McGregor, Mrs. Massey, Mrs. Monro, Miss Morris, Mrs. H. Roberts, Mr. Wilde, Mr. Herbert Woods, and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins. THE FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN. An interesting address on the general work of the Society was delivered by Mr. SUMNER, from the London head office. He pointed that in the Warrington District although this year they had a smaller population to be worked by one inspector, yet they had, within a very small number, as many cases as in the previous year. That showed that given a certain population, there was a certain proportion of cases to he found. The more inspectors they had, the more cases would be discovered. Throughout the county there had been 560 more cases than last year, although the cost of working had been substantially reduced. Contradicting the idea that the N.S.P.C.C. was a persecuting society whose object WAS to send parents to prison and children to the Workhouse, he pointed out that out of 38,000 cases dealt with in only 2,300 had there been prosecu- tions. Two children out of every live who came under the inspectors' observation were known to be insured. A question for the immediate future would be that of checking or regulating infant insurance, which, accord- ing to Coroners, proved a great temptation to many mothers. Dealing with the subject of underfed school children, Mr. Stunner said that in nine cases out of ten the parents could feed their children, but unfortunately they did not. If only parental responsibility could be brought home, if the Act of Parliament under which that Society worked could be enforced throughout the country ss it ought to be, they would hear very little of the complaints of children going to school without sufficient food. Mr. Sumner asked all his hearers to do their Utmost for the success of the forthcoming bazaar at Chester which would form part of the national special effort to raise £50,000. They wanted 100 additional inspectors before the Society could fully carry out its duty. A resolution commending the work of the Society to the support of the humane patriotic public was passed on the motion of the chairman seconded by Mr. Jenkins who suggested that clergy and pastors might devote more attention to impressing upon parents their duties in life, as well as teaching children the lessons of obedience and respect. Votes of thanks to the Mayor for the use of the Council Chamber and to the Chairman for presiding concluded the meeting.

A few yards from the bridge stood a cab awaiting us. Two of the constables, myself, and the woman stepped into it, leaving the other to await the result of the night watchman's search for the body. My timidity on first boarding the car was nothing compsr•d to my present terror. This was my first contact with the law in person, and I was awed. I shall never forget that journey. The poor woman shrank into a corner of the cab like a terrified creature. As we passed the street lamps and they threw their sickly light into the recesses of the vehicle, I could see her face, pale as death. When we arrived at the police station, she had to be helped into the superintendent's office, for she was very near to total collapse. She was accommodated with a chair, whilst I was ordered to repeat my information "You saw the woman throw the child from the car into the river ?" "Yes." You are perfectly certain of that ?" "Yes ; she threw it through the open window, just before we arrived at this side of the river." " It is not possible for you to have been dozing, I suppose, and have dream t such a thing,"said the officious cleric. THE INQUEST.

Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, conducted the inquest at the Police Station on Monday evening. Mary Ellen Dennett, 25, Egerton-street said her husband and son left home at 6.45 on Saturday evening to go for a walk. Later she heard that they had been drowned in the Mersey and she saw them when they were brought home dead. Before going out her husbana said be intended to visit Grappenhall,but he never men- tined that he intended to go on the river. Sergeant Barlow (coroner's officer) said that by cross- ing the river by boat deceased would have made the journey to Grappenhall about two miles shorter. Ellen Hayes, widow, 15, Egerton street, said the deceased Arthur Tomlinson washer sonand was 23 years of age. She last saw him alive at 6.15 on Saturday evening when he left home in his usual good health. He was a very good swimmer and a member of the Howley Rowing Club. George Platt, boat proprietor, 30,Howley Lane,stated that at 7 o'clock on Saturday evening he was at the boathouse at Howley when the two Dennetts came to him. Dennett, senior, asked him to row them across I the river as they wanted to go to Latchford. Tomlinson however, volunteered to take them across and they started in a boat which was registered to carry three. At 7.30 witness heard that the boat had capsized and all the occupants had been drowned. Tomlinson was a member of the Rowing Club and an expert swimmer. In answer to the Coroner, witness said that a skiff like the deceased were rowing in required very careful handling. The boat was a new one this season. Wit- ness agreed with the Coroner that if the occupants tried to change their seats the skiff would capsize easily. Frederick Kelly, general labourer, 2, Academy Place, said that at 7.30 on Saturday evening he was on the river bank at Howley and saw the deceased in a boat 13 mid-stream. At that time his attention happened to be drawn to something else, but on hearing a splash he looked round and saw the three occupants in the water and the boat bottom upwards. He was unable to swim so he gave the alarm to the lock-keeper,but the deceased sank before assistance reached them. Witness did not see any changing of seats. John Rathbone, lock keeper, Paddington Lock, said that at 7.30 on Saturday evening he was at the lock when he heard that three persons had been drowned in the river. He procuied grappling irons and, with assistance, recovered all the bodies. The boat was overturned, and the fact that the oars had been taken out of the rowlocks and placed at the bottom of the boat (where they were found) appeared to indicate that the deceased had been trying to change seats. The Coroner said that from the legal standpoint the case was a very simple one, but apart from that, he felt certain that such an occurrence must have cast a gloom over the borough of Warrington. However callous and indifferent one might become, it was impossible to hear such a narrative without feeling very sad and honestly sorry that three valuable lives had been lost under such circumstances. He had had a great deal of experience of rowing in his younger days and he _ knew that one had to be very careful in handling em ail skiffs. Such accidents as the present one invariably happened when people were trying to change seats in mid-stream in a craft of that description. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidentally drowned," the foreman (Mr. F. J. Whittle) announcing that they desired to express their sympathy with the relatives in their tragic bereavement. THE FUNERAL Impressive scenes were witnessed at the funeral of the victims of the fatality which took place at the Cemetery on Tuesday afternoon, and there was striking evidence of the sympathy which has been aroused: throughout the town. A service was held at the Parish Church at 2.30, and was conducted by the Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis), assisted by the Revs. T. W. Barber, T. A. Ridpath and G. A. Dearden, a large number of relatives and friends attending. As the mourners left the church, the organist played the Dead March ia " Saul." A procession was formed to the cemetery. and was headed by officers and members of the Ancient Order of Foresters representing all the Courts, the late Mr. Dennett having been a member of that society. The remains of Mr. Dennett and son were first hid to rest, the service being conducted by the Rector, while Brother Chrirnes, P.D.C.R,, also took part in it. The Rector then conducted the service over Mr. Tomlinson - Deceased was a member of the Rowing Club, and was employed at Messrs. Richmond and Co.'., and both the club and the firm were fairly represented. The floral tributes included two from the-Rowing Club,an artificial and a natural one from the employees of Messrs. Rich- mond, and one each from the workmen of Messrs. c(R‘ovv ulaarrtnr Bros. n‘s gjtanz‘l? TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL. --- A special meeting of the Trades and Labour Counci was held on Tuesday. Mr. Podruore presided over good attendance of delegates. THE UNEMPLOYED. A letter was read from the Warrington branch of the Independent Labour Party in Ating the Council to co-operate with them and other Labour organization? in holding a demonstration in favour of the Unemployo; Rill being passed into law during the present session 01 Parliament and to press for amendments to the Bill. Mr. ‘VILKINSON (Engineers) asked what form of 11' demonstration it was proposed to hold. Councillor SToTr in answer to the question stated that in many large centres it was proposed to have pro- cessions through the principal streets with bands nod trade union banners,to be followed by a public meeting. In some towns there would be in addition a processioll of unemployed workmen who would be provided with 5 dinner or tea. As far as Warrington was concerned be; Mr. Stott, was afraid we could hardly attempt to bold a demonstration on such a large scale. In his opinioll a large open-air meeting would be sufficient. The details, however, would be left to the Joirt Committee. It was unanimously decided to co-operate with the LL.P. Councillor Stott and the vice-president, Mr. Plumb, were elected as delegates to act on behalf of the Council.

AMMONIA and PHOSPHATES, this must strike you as to Its being far away superior to the ordinary Blood and Bone Compound Manure made by other processes. THE EXAMINER. JUNE 3rd. 1905.

LIBERAL VICTORY AT WHITBY. --- Polling took place in the Whitby Division on Thurs- day for the election of a member of Parliament to fill the vacancy caused by the succession of Mr. Ernest Beckett, Conservative, to the peerage. The candidates were Mr. Gervase Beckett, Conservative, and Mr. Noel Buxton, Liberal. The poll was declared yesterday (Friday) as follows : Mr. Noel Buxton (L) 4547 Mr. Gervase Beckett (C) 4102 Majority.. .445 The figures at previous elections were 1885. 1892. E. W. Beckett (C) 5,049 E. W. Beckett (C)... 4,909 Arthur Pease (L) Conservative maj 340 Conservative maj. 1,083 1886. 1895. E. W. Beckett (C) 5.078 E. W. Beckett,unopposed J. M. Clayhills (L) E. W. Beckett,anopposa Conservative maj. 1,138 NOTES AND NOTIONS. The people of Runcorn and Widnes were Oar:spree -',With delight on Monday. * * * It has been remarked rather cruelly that the only fatal shot fired by the late Baltic fleet was the one which .1"- 'sunk a British fishing boat on the Dogger Bank. , * * * "Every human being has a right to find, according ' lo his taste, the way of social happiness as well as to Paradise, and to show it to his fellow men. No matter whether he is right or wrong, people who hear 'him will decide for themselves."—Stepniak. * ' The Runcorn Rural District Council and Board of Guardians have a reputation for the 'expeditious dis- Patch of business, but on Tuesday all records were broken. The meetings of both authorities together lasted barely an hour. Proliably the fact that an ex- . • eellent luncheon (provided by Mr. S. Taylor, J.P,) awaited the members in another room dispelled any desire to debate at length. * * * Councillor Pemberton seems determined to get at something in connection with the Water Committee's contracts, and his pushfulness is extraordinary.. On the agenda for next week's Council meeting he has several pertinent questions re the bill for work and extras at Winwick,and also a couple of special motions, one instructing the Engineer to disclose all papers, letteis &c., in connection with the contract at Delph- lane, and the other appointing a committee to inquire into " the very large amount of extras." * * * Victoria Park bowling green still looks very trim and tempting, but it is not yet in use. Sonic people '• aver that it never will be to any large extent, but these folk must be pessimists. For some days now the committee has been engaged in erecting a sub- stantial-looking pavilion at a cost of £l6B, anct we can ' only hope that dere will be some revenue in return for the large expenditure already incurred on the t" venture. 'Meanwhile a sub-committee is to consider rules and regulations, and fix the date of opening. Oh! these sub-committees ! Some of them twiddle their thumbs while time flies. * * * The amateur photographer who desires something new on which to practise his art will find a valuable suggestion in a recent " pastoral " issued by the Bishop of Chester. The worthy prelate writes : "0, that it were possible to photograph a selfish soul, and by the exhibition of its shrivelled and distorted ttgliness, to open its possessor's eyes and shame him into wholesome living and laudable service !" What queer negatives would be developed if this were Possible ! * * , The residents of Standish. near Wigan, evidently know very little of military matters. On Saturday half-a-dozen Warrington Volunteer signallers marched -through the village on their way to the cyclists manceuvres, and the excitement was intense. The Populace turned out in full force to see the men march past, and one youngster was heard to comment to a companion "I say Billy, they're off playing cricket; •- Yon chap has th' wickets." The "wickets" were the legS of a telescope tripod. ** * • . The Bishop of Peterborough has been warning his Clergy against scandal-loving women in their parishes saying that the talking of these " old cats "i often led to serious dissensions in a parish. It reminds me (says X.Y.Z. in the "Daily News ") of a story of the Presence of mind showed by a clergyman's wife. One of the gossips of the parish was visiting her, and the husband left the house in despair. Returning an hour later, he called out to his wife, " I suppose that old oat' has gone, dear ?" The gossip had not departed, but the clergyman's wife had the wit to reply, Yes, ray dear, I sent it home in a basket this mormng ! " • * The Parks Committee of the Warrington Corpora- -1O ir would do well to emulate the example of the Accrington Corporation. Plants have been sent out to the various day schools in the borough, for the 'scholars to cultivate at their own homes. Later in ,the year a competition will be held, when prizes will ue given for the best specimens brought in. We are inclined to think that such action would be far more Profitable and helpful than the unfortunate quibblings and sectarian jealousies which have recently cropped 113. When will some of our representatives realise that there is still much progressive work to do and that children may be benefited, by a display of ordin- -417 commonsense ? . .

TWELVE MONTFIS POrt A DANGEROUS VA GAB9ND• An unusual charge was preferred at St. Helens oa Thursday against a man named James Dillon, who was dealt with under Section 7 of the Prevention of Crimea Act, 1871. The Chief Constable said that on Saturday, the 20th inst., Mrs. Webster, of 131, Prescot-road, 103 going along Westfield-street with her purse in her left hand, when she met prisoner, and he tried to snatch the purse, but failed. Mrs. Webster followed him doe?! the street, and gave him in charge. Dillon then butted the officer in the face, knocked him down, and kicked him. During the struggle he bit a piece out of tbe officer's left thurab:—Dillon was sent to gaol for twelve' months' hard labour.

Now is the time at the seaside when many astonishing habits and practices can be noticed among cyclists of both sexes. These include the carrying on the bicycle of parasols, tennis- racquets, cricket-bats, pet dogs, &c., and they are no doubt interesting ways of demonstrating the rider's familiarity with the wheel, and though quite harmless when indulged in by riders of sufficient skill, are certainly not to be realm- mended. Wood Rims and Rim-Brakes. Now that wood rims are so common on light road mounts it is well to call attention to the fact that good wood rims present no drawback to the use of rim-brakes. The brake shoes employed should not be too harsh or hard in substance, or there will be appreciable wear of the rim. The writer has used a rim-brake on a front wheel wood rim for more then a year without any sign of injury to the rim. Nor has there been more than average wear of the rubber shoe, which acts as smoothly and silently as on a steel rim. In default of a hub- brake in the rear wheel, it is possible to have a wood rim sheathed with metal to take the friction of a powerful rim-brake. Two brakes are essential where a free-wheel is used, as it is obviously dan- gerous to depend on one brake in descending long hills when without the aid of back pedalling. Properly fitted, a Bowden brake may be relied upon to uphold its repuation for efficiency under any circumstances. The Ecco brake, sent out lw the Eadie Manufacturing Co., is particularly suited fop use on wood rims of even the narrowest pattern.
